Log into your Chase Ultimate Rewards account and your Southwest Rapid Rewards account and tally your existing points. You need 135,000 qualifying Rapid Rewards points earned in a single calendar year to trigger the Companion Pass. Note: points already sitting in your Rapid Rewards account from prior years don't count — you need to earn 135,000 in the target calendar year.

The optimal window is October through December of the prior year. Apply for Southwest credit cards (personal and/or business) so that your signup bonuses post to your Rapid Rewards account in January. Verify with Southwest's current card offers — signup bonuses of 50,000-75,000 points are common. A couple can each apply for a card; a solo pursuer can apply for both a personal and a business card if they have qualifying self-employment income.

Call Chase or Southwest after your first statement closes to confirm when points will post. Timing is everything.

Once your signup bonuses post, calculate how many points you still need. If you're short:

Southwest will notify you by email once you've crossed 135,000 qualifying points. Log into your Rapid Rewards account at southwest.com and navigate to "My Account" → "Companion Pass" to confirm the benefit is active.

You can designate — and change — your companion up to three times per calendar year. Name your most frequent travel partner first. Go to southwest.com, log in, and find the Companion Pass section to add their name and contact information.

Searching is simple: go to southwest.com and search any route. Toggle between "$ Dollars" and "Rapid Rewards Points" to see your cost. When you book with points and your companion is designated, Southwest will automatically prompt you to add your companion to the same flight. Your companion pays taxes only — typically $5.60 each way for domestic flights.

Pro tip: Southwest opens its booking window 330 days in advance. For peak travel like spring break or holiday weeks, set a calendar reminder and book the day the window opens.

The Companion Pass works because Southwest treats it as a status benefit rather than a points redemption. Most airline companion programs require you to burn miles for the second ticket or limit usage to specific fare classes. Southwest flips this model: once you earn the pass, your companion flies on every single flight you take — paid or award — for the remainder of the qualifying year plus the entire following calendar year. The only cost is taxes. Because Southwest has no blackout dates, no fuel surcharges, and no change fees, the pass amplifies in value with every trip you take. The January-earning calendar arbitrage is the key optimization that turns a good benefit into an extraordinary one.

Let's make this real. It's March, and your family of four wants to spend a week in Orlando this July — theme parks, pool time, the works.

A quick search on southwest.com shows round-trip flights from Chicago Midway to Orlando for $320 per person. For four tickets, that's $1,280 cash. Standard family travel math: painful but doable.

You have the Companion Pass active, earned back in January. You book two tickets: yours at 22,000 Rapid Rewards points (worth ~$308 at 1.4 cents per point), and your spouse's ticket at $5.60 in taxes as your designated companion. Your two kids? You use 44,000 more points for their tickets. Total out-of-pocket: 66,000 points + $11.20 in taxes.

Let's value those 66,000 points at their Chase transfer cost — you transferred them 1:1 from Chase Ultimate Rewards, where they were worth roughly $825 in travel via the portal. But by pairing them with the Companion Pass, your spouse flies free, effectively doubling your points' purchasing power on this trip.

Cash price: $1,280. Your cost: 66,000 points + $11.20. That's a CPP of roughly 1.9 cents per point on the base redemption — but accounting for your companion flying free, the effective CPP on the entire trip rockets to 3.8 cents per point. Well within the 1.5–5.9 cent range where this sweet spot lives.

And here's what makes Southwest specifically wonderful for families: no change fees. Book in March, change in June when your kid's baseball tournament schedule shifts, rebook without penalty. No one in the FI community is paying $200-per-person change fees.

The experience itself? Economy class on Southwest, open seating, snacks, and friendly crew. It's not lie-flat business class to Tokyo — but that's not the point. The point is your family of four flies to Florida for a week, and the flights barely register on your annual budget. That's FI-level travel.

Time your credit card applications so signup bonuses post in January of your target year. A bonus that posts January 5th gives you until December 31st of the following year to use the Companion Pass — nearly 24 months of 2-for-1 flights. This single timing decision can mean the difference between 13 months and 23 months of value.

The Rapid Rewards Shopping Portal (rapidrewardsshopping.com) awards points on purchases at hundreds of retailers. Even after you've earned the Companion Pass, continuing to earn Rapid Rewards points means more flights at lower point costs. Treat it like a cashback portal and check it before any online purchase.

Southwest opens availability 330 days in advance. For Thanksgiving, Christmas, spring break, and summer weekends in popular markets, set a calendar reminder and book the exact morning the window opens. Southwest doesn't have blackout dates, but high-demand flights sell out in Wanna Get Away fare class — the cheapest points tier — quickly.

You can change your designated companion up to three times per calendar year. If your life situation changes — a different travel partner, a new relationship, a child aging into a separate ticket — use this flexibility. Just don't burn all three changes frivolously.

Purchased points do not count toward the 135,000 qualifying threshold for the Companion Pass. Don't make this mistake when you're close to the threshold — transfer from Chase instead.

Some people try to earn the Companion Pass in November or December. You'll get it for only a few weeks before the calendar resets. The math simply doesn't work. January earning is the only strategy worth pursuing.

The Southwest Rapid Rewards Performance Business Credit Card (or similar business cards) have separate signup bonuses that stack with personal card bonuses. If you have any self-employment income — freelancing, a side hustle, a rental property — you likely qualify. This is often the fastest path to 135,000 points for a single applicant.

Free calculators, a personalized FI roadmap, and a community of 25K+ on the same journey.

The Southwest Companion Pass was practically invented for the FI-minded family. If you have a consistent travel partner — a spouse, a partner, a parent you visit annually — and you fly domestically more than two or three times a year, this benefit will transform your travel budget.

Minimum points to get started: You need to earn 135,000 qualifying Rapid Rewards points in a single calendar year. In practice, this means you either need access to Southwest credit card signup bonuses (which requires good to excellent credit, typically 700+) or a substantial existing balance of Chase Ultimate Rewards points to transfer at 1:1.

How it fits the FI strategy: The Companion Pass is a force multiplier. Every dollar you would have spent on a second ticket stays in your investment accounts instead. Over a two-year Companion Pass window, a couple who takes six domestic trips per year could easily bank $2,000-$4,000 in travel savings — real money that compounds.

Who this is NOT for: Solo travelers get zero benefit from the Companion Pass. International travelers who primarily fly non-Southwest routes won't find much use here. And if you're averse to economy class or require a specific airline's premium cabin, this redemption isn't your path.

The Southwest Companion Pass earns a **moderate difficulty rating** — and that rating is almost entirely about the *earning*, not the booking. Once you have the Companion Pass, booking is literally the simplest award experience in travel rewards: search southwest.com, find your flight, click "Add Companion." There are no partner airlines to navigate, no availability calendars to decode, no call centers to endure. It's a 5/5 on ease of booking for a reason. The moderate rating comes from **three earning challenges**: **1. The credit requirement.** Southwest credit cards require good to excellent credit. If you're early in your credit-building journey, this strategy may need to wait 12-18 months while you strengthen your profile. **2. The timing discipline.** Earning the pass is straightforward, but earning it in January takes planning. You need to apply for cards 2-3 months in advance and coordinate signup bonus posting dates — something most people have never had to think about. **3. The 135,000-point threshold.** This is a real number. It requires intentional credit card strategy, not casual spending. For a single applicant without a business card, it may require supplementing with Chase transfers or portal activity. **How to overcome these challenges:** - Start planning in **October or November** for a January earn target - Check whether you qualify for a Southwest business card — it dramatically simplifies reaching the threshold - Use the Rapid Rewards Shopping Portal year-round to passively accumulate qualifying points If you've successfully applied for a travel rewards credit card before and you have a consistent travel partner, you can absolutely do this. The ChooseFI community has helped hundreds of families earn the Companion Pass — and not one of them regretted the effort.

Open a personal and/or business Southwest credit card so that signup bonuses of 50,000 to 75,000 points each will post to your Rapid Rewards account in January. Call to confirm when points will post after your first statement closes.

Once signup bonuses post, calculate your gap. Transfer Chase Ultimate Rewards at 1:1 (instant) to fill any shortfall. Use the Rapid Rewards Shopping Portal for additional earning on everyday purchases.

After Southwest confirms your Companion Pass, log in to southwest.com and designate your travel partner. Book flights as normal — Southwest will prompt you to add your companion for taxes only ($5.60 each way domestic).

Set calendar reminders for Thanksgiving, Christmas, spring break, and summer travel. Southwest opens its booking window 330 days in advance. Book the morning the window opens to lock in Wanna Get Away fares at the lowest point costs.

If you earn it on January 2nd, you get nearly 24 months of 2-for-1 flights. If you earn it in November, you get roughly 14 months. This is why timing your earning to January is the critical optimization.

These 10 New Features Are Coming to iOS 27.3

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Apple dropped the first developer beta for iOS 27.3 on Tuesday. While this update isn't as large as iOS 27 proper, it's surprisingly feature-filled for a 0.3 update. This is only a beta, so anything could (and likely will) change before Apple officially launches the update. But, as it stands, iOS 27.3 tests 10 new features—including one feature the company trialed in the previous beta.

Technically, anyone with a compatible iPhone can try out iOS 27.3 right now—assuming you enroll your Apple Account in the developer program. But a word of warning: Betas can be temperamental, and could be risky. As such, I'd recommend avoiding the beta until Apple releases the public beta, which comes after a bit more testing to iron out larger issues. Either way, make sure to make a full backup of your iPhone to a Mac or PC first, as issues with the beta could result in permanent data loss.

With watchOS 27, Apple released "Audio Intelligence," a new set of features for Apple Watch Series 12 and Ultra 4. Audio Intelligence uses the watches' new S11 chip, along with on-device AI and cloud-based AI, to run new features, like Sound Recognition and Shazam (music recognition).

With iOS 27.3, however, the company is now officially testing the other two Audio Intelligence features it originally announced with watchOS 27. First, there's Live Rewind: By double-clicking the watch crown, you can pull up a transcript of the last 15 seconds of a conversation. Siri Recap, on the other hand, displays summaries of conversations you had that day, in case you need a refresher on any chats you might have otherwise forgotten about,

The company generated some controversy when it first announced these two Audio Intelligence features, since, on the surface, it sounds like these new Apple Watches are essentially spying on people all day long. According to Apple, however, that isn't really the case. The company says Audio Intelligence is private, and never records or stores any audio. No speakers are ever identified, and text results (both transcripts and summaries) are deleted automatically after a set period of time unless the user saves them to the Siri app. You can read more about how Audio Intelligence works here.

Despite their claims, I'm sure there will still be strong feelings surrounding these Audio Intelligence features. We'll have to see whether Apple can prove Live Rewind and Siri Recap aren't all that creepy throughout the iOS 27.3 beta process.

iOS 27.3 also introduces app actions for Siri AI. Apple is testing new abilities for Siri AI, including organizing files, planning events, or adding photos to albums. You could ask Siri to organize your Downloads folder, or delete files that haven't been opened in more than a year. You could ask Siri to find a coffee shop you went to last week, or to save an article to your Reading List in Safari. You could ask Siri to check your calendar and set up a time for an event based on when you're free. You can even ask the assistant to schedule, edit, or unsend messages.

For example, you could ask Siri AI to help you plan a trip with your friends. You could ask the AI to put together a themed playlist in Apple Music, schedule a message to a group about an upcoming trip, organize the websites you've been visiting in Safari into a tab group, and, after the trip, ask Siri to move your best photos to your favorites album. These new actions work in the following apps: Calendar, Files, Finder, Journal, Mail, Maps, Messages, Music, Notes, Photos, Preview, Reminders, Safari, Shortcuts, and Weather. Developers can also add Siri AI app actions support to their own apps.

Apple started testing its new Health app with the iOS 27.2 beta, but pulled the redesign before the official release. Now, with iOS 27.3, the new app is back. The new Health app has a health dashboard for the first time, as well as new features like Health Age and movement assessments. It seems to be pulling from some popular competitor services, hopefully, for the better. For more on the new Health app, check out Lifehacker health editor Beth Skwarecki's explainer.

Continuing on the Health track, iOS 27.3 also introduces "Gym Strength." This is a new Fitness+ feature that offers a personalized four-week strength-training plan. You can customize these plans to meet your goals, experience level, and schedule, and they adapt as you progress. You'll get recommendations based on your experience with exercises, reps, and sets, including video demos for exercises. You can access Gym Strength on both iPhone and iPad, and Apple Watches are optional—though you can log your progress with the wearable if you have it.

Vertical tabs have been a favorite among browser aficionados, as they give you more screen real estate in your browser while keeping your tabs off the side. Apple hasn't made the jump—until now. This feature works with iPads running iPadOS 27.3 as well as Macs running macOS 27.3.

Another new Safari feature in testing is "Adaptive Dark Mode." This feature forces websites into a dark theme, if they don't have a native theme of their own. There are third-party apps that can do this, but this is the first time Apple has offered this feature built into Safari.

Apple is also testing new StandBy experiences with iOS 27.3, which were previously only available on the iPhone Duo. When you turn your iPhone sideways while plugged in, you'll find new Analog and Digital Clock faces with new customization options. There's also a new Photos face, with the option to pull images from Shared Albums.

"Reachability" pulls your iPhone's display down towards the bottom of the screen, so you can access items towards the top of the display with one hand. It's a useful feature for those of us with big iPhones, and now, there's no iPhone bigger than the Duo. With iOS 27.3, Apple is testing Reachability for its first foldable phone. It's a bit like the inverse of how Reachability currently works: You can swipe on the left or right side of your Duo's display to shift the other side of the screen towards your current position. If you're holding your Duo in your left hand, a swipe of the left side will pull the right side over to the left, and vice versa.

Apple is also rolling out new languages for Siri AI in iOS 27.3. Now, Apple's smart assistant supports Traditional Chinese, Vietnamese, Turkish, and Norwegian.

If you have an Apple Intelligence-compatible iPad running iPadOS 27.3, you now have access to Siri Mode in Camera. As with iOS, you can open the Camera app on iPadOS to access Siri Mode, and ask Siri about your surroundings.

Liverpool are reportedly in a strong position to sign Monaco midfielder Lamine Camara as Andoni Iraola looks to strengthen his midfield options during the January transfer window.

According to a report from Football Insider, Monaco are now willing to negotiate a potential departure for the Senegal international, while the player is also open to discussing a move to Anfield. The Reds have maintained their interest after monitoring his situation throughout the summer.

The 22-year-old has established himself as one of the most promising young midfielders in French football, attracting attention with his energy, technical ability and impressive performances for Monaco.

Liverpool are keen to add another quality central midfielder to their squad, and Camara could provide greater balance and dynamism in the middle of the park. His ability to contribute defensively while also progressing the ball would make him an attractive option for Iraola.

The Senegalese international came close to joining Chelsea during the summer transfer window, with the Blues reportedly agreeing a deal worth £47 million. However, Monaco eventually pulled out of the negotiations after another proposed departure from their squad failed to materialise.

Chelsea have now shifted their attention towards alternative midfield targets, potentially leaving Liverpool in a favourable position to complete the transfer.

Want more CaughtOffside coverage? Add us as a preferred source on Google to your favourites list for news you can trust.

However, Liverpool will still need to negotiate an agreement with the French club. Monaco are reportedly looking for a fee in the region of £50 million to sanction his departure in January.

The Ligue 1 side are keen to generate significant funds after failing to offload Camara during the previous transfer window. Their financial situation could therefore work in Liverpool’s favour.

The midfielder has also remained an important member of the squad, featuring consistently in Ligue 1 this season and demonstrating his importance to the team.

Liverpool are expected to continue discussions over a potential move, with January offering an opportunity to secure one of their long-term midfield targets. Although negotiations could still prove complicated, the Reds appear well placed to make progress in the transfer race.

Safe Word: The Best Jewelry Launches of November 2026

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Safe Word is a series highlighting the latest trends in the jewelry market, keeping you up to date on all things that sparkle and shine.

November’s jewelry launches are looking both backward and forward, with designers revisiting their roots, reworking archival ideas, and stepping into entirely new territory. First up, Pomellato reaches back to 1997 to revive “Moneta Orientale” as a colorful modern talisman; David Yurman celebrates the magic of a New York holiday with Michael B. Jordan, Yerin Ha, and Inde Navarrette; Vhernier revisits its iconic “Trottola” design with new proportions and contrasting materials; Di Volpe reinterprets the classic tennis bracelet with customizable gemstones in “DiSegno”; and Brent Neale transforms the classic bow into sculptural fine jewelry with “Wrap It Up.” From archival revivals and meaningful motifs to innovative designs and new creative directions, November’s launches prove that sometimes the best way forward starts with looking back. For the rest of the month, we’ve got you covered on all the jewelry-related happenings worth knowing about.

Pomellato Revives Its 1997 Moneta Orientale Pendant as a Colorful Talisman

Pomellato is looking to its archives for its latest good-luck charm. For the newest chapter of “Memorie d’Archivio,” the maison revives Moneta Orientale, a 1997 pendant inspired by the ancient symbolism of a circle surrounding a square, representing the harmony between heaven and earth. Made in 33 stone-and-pavé combinations, the design pairs colorful hard stones including black jade, amazonite, sodalite, rhodochrosite, and labradorite with Pomellato’s signature irregular pavé of diamonds, sapphires, rubies, and more. Offered in two proportions, each pendant transforms the archival motif into a modern talisman for prosperity and protection.

Bibi van der Velden Makes Her High Jewelry Debut With ‘Eclipse’

Bibi van der Velden is entering a new era with “Eclipse,” the designer’s first high jewelry collection and most refined exploration of diamonds to date. Inspired by the fleeting moment when light gives way to darkness, the collection includes two six-piece suites: one in white diamonds and another defined by an ombré of diamonds into gray spinels deepening into black. Van der Velden’s signature alligator motif is abstracted into fluid, sculptural silhouettes, with scales engraved into the white gold on the reverse of each piece. “With Eclipse, I wanted to take those familiar elements and transform them into something more abstract and sculptural,” she says. “It’s a collection about transformation, which has always been at the heart of my work.”

Aflalo and Leandra Medine Cohen Turn Everyday Indulgences Into Fine Jewelry

Aflalo is finding the humor in our obsession with wellness with “The Vanities,” a new two-piece fine jewelry capsule designed exclusively by Leandra Medine Cohen. Playing with the contradiction between longevity and life’s little indulgences, the collection transforms two unlikely everyday accessories into precious objects. The Memento Mori is a 14-karat yellow gold cigar band set with more than four carats of diamonds, designed to slip over an Oura Ring, while The Libertine is a sterling silver pendant case adorned with diamonds and a wild-haired woman outlined in gold, designed to hold nicotine pouches or other small essentials. Equal parts extravagant and irreverent, the collection makes a playful case for enjoying life’s pleasures without sacrificing personal style.

Julia Berolzheimer’s Hart Jewelry Collection Draws on Childhood Memories

Hart is teaming up with Julia Berolzheimer for a new jewelry collection that celebrates the art of the stack. Inspired by Berolzheimer’s childhood memories of her grandmother’s California ranch and her collection of Art Deco minaudières, the collaboration brings together whimsical charms, colorful gemstones, and meaningful talismans designed to be mixed, layered, and restyled. Botanical motifs take shape in Tree of Life coins, daisies, bees, and fruit-inspired charms, while carnelian, turquoise, and pink opal add playful pops of color. One standout is the Perfume Vesta Case, a modern interpretation of a Victorian-era accessory that can hold a sample-sized fragrance and hang alongside other charms. With interchangeable chains, cords, and pendants, the collection makes a case for jewelry that feels just as personal as the memories behind it.

David Yurman Captures the Magic of a New York Holiday

David Yurman is celebrating the magic of the holidays in New York City with a new campaign featuring global brand ambassador Michael B. Jordan alongside actresses Yerin Ha and Inde Navarrette. Set against a snow-covered Washington Square Park, the campaign captures a series of quintessential New York moments, from Jordan and Ha facing off in a spirited card game to Navarrette hailing a cab in search of the perfect holiday gift. Throughout the campaign, the cast wears some of the house’s most recognizable designs, including 18-karat gold Cable bracelets and pieces from the “Starburst” and “Armory” collections. Directed by Bradford Young and photographed by Drew Vickers, the campaign brings together the spirit of the city, the joy of togetherness, and the timeless appeal of David Yurman’s jewelry.

Vhernier Reintroduces Its Iconic ‘Trottola’ Design With New Proportions

Vhernier is revisiting one of its most recognizable designs with a new interpretation of “Trottola,” the sculptural collection originally introduced in 2003. Inspired by the continuous motion of a spinning top, the latest designs play with proportions, contrasting materials, and asymmetrical shapes across rings, bracelets, necklaces, and earrings. Polished rose gold meets glossy black jet, with select pieces featuring diamond pavé for an added contrast of light and dark. The collection also focuses on versatility, from rings designed for stacking to convertible earclips that can be worn as studs or dangling earrings. With its fluid forms and unexpected material combinations, “Trottola” brings a fresh perspective to Vhernier’s signature sculptural approach to jewelry.

Di Volpe is rethinking the classic tennis bracelet with “DiSegno,” a new customizable fine jewelry system designed to change with its wearer. Using concealed, patent-pending connections, individual cultured diamonds and gemstones, called Elements, can be removed, rearranged, or added by hand, allowing wearers to transform bracelets into necklaces or earrings without compromising the seamless look of traditional tennis jewelry. The collection launches with 28 stone-and-cut combinations, including rubies, emeralds, sapphires, and diamonds, alongside a limited-edition collaboration with Lebanese artist Ali Cha’aban featuring five sculptural designs. With new cuts, colors, and artist collaborations planned for future releases, “DiSegno” makes the case for fine jewelry that’s never quite finished.

Brent Neale Riffs on the Bow in Fine Jewelry

Brent Neale is putting a bow on the holiday season with “Wrap It Up,” a new fine jewelry collection that transforms the familiar ribbon motif into sculptural works of art. Drawing inspiration from Old Hollywood glamour and the femininity of the 1960s, the designer plays with the contrast between rigid precious metals and the soft movement of fabric, crafting bows that twist, curl, and unfurl in 18-karat gold and platinum. Colored gemstones sit nestled within the ribbon-like forms, while pavé diamonds accentuate every curve and fold. Equal parts playful and precious, the collection brings a little gift-wrapped glamour to the jewelry box.

Grace Winfrey’s ‘Poppy Fortuna’ Collection Reimagines Good-Luck Charms

Grace Winfrey is bringing a little extra luck to the jewelry box with “Poppy Fortuna,” a new fine jewelry collection inspired by the natural beauty of the Italian landscape and symbols of good fortune. Scarlet poppies, sunflowers, bees, four-leaf clovers, horseshoes, and ladybugs come to life through vibrant gemstones and intricate pavé, creating miniature mosaics of color and texture. Highlights include a Lucky Bangle combining three good-luck charms, sculptural sunflower earrings with tiny ladybugs tucked into their petals, and layered charm necklaces designed to feel like modern keepsakes. Handcrafted in New York City, each piece transforms familiar symbols of prosperity, protection, and happiness into playful, colorful works of art.

Robinson Pelham Channels ’90s Glamour for Its Anniversary

Robinson Pelham is closing out its 30th anniversary celebrations with “Suite 96,” a new campaign and collection launch inspired by the glamour of late-’90s London. Set inside a private suite at St. James’s Hotel & Club in Mayfair, the campaign considers jewelry the ultimate room service, with diamonds arriving on trays, gold spilling out of wardrobes, and plenty of pieces to layer before a night out. At the center is “Studio 30,” a four-piece anniversary collection featuring diamond necklaces and statement earrings, including the sculptural Via and Hira necklaces, cascading diamond drops, and the bold Solstice Supreme. Alongside the anniversary designs, the house introduces new collections and additions to existing favorites, from chunky gold links and graphic heart rings to colorful enamel and gemstone combinations. Together, the launches celebrate three decades of Robinson Pelham’s bold, playful approach to fine jewelry.

Le Vian and Jared Revive Georgian-Era Romance

Le Vian is bringing a little old-world romance to Jared Jewelers with its new exclusive “Georgian Chocolate & Nude Diamonds” collection. Inspired by the candlelit jewels of the Georgian era, when jewelry was designed to capture and reflect the warmth of natural light, the collection rethinks historic buttercup settings in polished Honey Gold, with eight rounded prongs framing each natural diamond. The designs pair Le Vian’s signature Chocolate and Nude Diamonds with warm gold detailing across solitaire rings, anniversary bands, tennis bracelets, necklaces, and earrings. Select styles introduce colorful gemstones including Chocolate Quartz, Green Apple Peridot, and Grape Amethyst, giving the vintage-inspired silhouettes a contemporary update. With its sculptural settings and warm-toned stones, the collection brings the romance of antique jewelry into the present.

Wempe’s ‘Au:thentic’ Celebrates the Art of Goldsmithing

Wempe’s new gender-inclusive fine jewelry collection, “Au:thentic,” puts craftsmanship front and center, finding inspiration in the foundational skills taught to aspiring goldsmiths. The collection takes its shape from the jump ring, one of the first techniques Patrick Sieroslawski learned as an apprentice more than 22 years ago, translating its form to necklaces, bracelets, rings, earrings, and pendants. Handcrafted at Wempe’s atelier in Germany, the pieces come in 18-karat yellow, white, and rose gold, with select styles set with diamonds. “Au:thentic” also marks the beginning of a multiyear rollout, with three additional chapters set to expand on its design language through twists, wraps, and interlocking links.

Decision intelligence for travel leaders.

Skift's capital tracker for early November 2026 shows travel's largest companies putting new money into two very different places: consolidation and shareholder returns. The headline transaction is Lufthansa Group's signed agreement with the Portuguese government to buy an initial 44.9% stake in TAP Air Portugal for €1.025 billion (about $1.19 billion), with the holding able to rise to 49.9% and the total to roughly €1.2 billion if employee-reserved shares are not taken up. Portugal keeps majority ownership, and closing requires regulatory approval. Everything else logged in the window is balance-sheet work rather than expansion: Marriott disclosed roughly $4 billion returned to shareholders through October 28 while expanding its revolving credit facility from $4.5 billion to $5 billion and issuing $1.25 billion in senior notes; Southwest Airlines closed a $1.25 billion two-tranche bond offering ahead of a November maturity; Ryanair authorized a new €800 million buyback to begin in February 2027 after completing a €750 million program in September; and aftermarket parts distributor AvAir closed $170 million in revolving credit facilities with a six-bank group to fund inventory and acquisitions. The pattern across the tracker is capital flowing to M&A, refinancing, and returns rather than to demand generation or distribution.

ChatGPT has hit roughly 20% of Google Search volume, OpenAI says

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

According to OpenAI, ChatGPT has managed to grow its search use to around 20% of Google Search’s total volume, a staggering figure.

Google Search has had a massively dominant lead in the search space, with Microsoft’s Bing a distant competitor with a single-digit share at any given time. But, following its push into AI-powered search about 2 years ago, OpenAI says that ChatGPT is catching up in a big way.

Speaking at an OpenAI press event/chat yesterday, ChatGPT consumer product lead Adam Fry confirmed that search volume in ChatGPT is “roughly one-fifth (20%) of the volume of queries of Google.”

There’s a lot of wiggle room there, as the source of Google’s search volume wasn’t revealed. OpenAI’s measurement of what a search query is boils down to when a user inputs a prompt/query that results in a web search – “Every time you ask a question and go search the web, that’s a search.” Again, a lot of wiggle room here.

But, in any case, this would be the biggest rival Google Search has had yet, and certainly explains Google’s massive push to expand AI in Search. Google admits it is facing “lower monetization” with that push, something OpenAI is also surely dealing with in its own efforts. The Verge notes that Fry said that “most people do not seem to mind ads” in ChatGPT and that “we could be doing this more, actually.”

Fry also said that “people really love” ChatGPT’s personalization and understanding.

The P2S, the X2D and H2C are all excellent models, and they've all seen big drops in price.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Black Friday is still weeks away, but Bambu Lab isn’t waiting around. Its sale page is already live and runs until 1 December. And there's some serious cash to be saved on some of its most capable multicolour 3D printers.

Our top pick is the Bambu Lab P2S for $649, down from $749. For that, you'll get a fully enclosed printer with a 256 x 256 x 256mm build volume, along with an AMS 2 Pro unit that handles multicolour printing and dries your filament too.

Want something even more advanced? The X2D and H2C have both dropped in price too, and I’ve compare all three below. Meanwhile, if you want to see what discounts other brands are offering right now, check the constantly updating price widgets in our best 3D printers guide.

This fully enclosed printer comes with an AMS 2 Pro for multicolour printing and filament drying, and offers a 256 x 256 x 256mm build volume.

The two nozzles mean faster multicolour printing and cleaner support removal. You also get a 300°C nozzle, a 65°C actively heated chamber and a 256 x 256 x 260mm build volume.

The H2C's hotend-swapping tech minimises purge waste. It comes with 350°C nozzles, a 65°C actively heated chamber, a 330 x 320 x 325mm build volume and an optional laser and cutting module.

All three of these bundles include an AMS 2 Pro and an enclosed printer with AI error detection, so the real question is how much multicolour work you do, and how demanding your materials are.

For most people, the P2S will be the best buy. It prints in multiple colours out of the box and handles materials such as ABS and ASA, and it’s $140 cheaper than the X2D. However, bear in mind it only has a single nozzle and lacks active chamber heating.

The X2D includes both, with essentially the same build volume, making it a smart choice if you often print with separate support material, while the 65°C chamber helps with tougher filaments. Just be aware that the auxiliary nozzle supports fewer filaments than the main one.

At the top end of the scale, in terms of both price and sophistication, is the H2C. It costs nearly three times as much as the P2S and weighs over twice as much at 32.5kg. But its hotend-swapping system slashes purge waste, and in our testing, models that’d take three days or more on an H2S took just over 24 hours on the H2C. If multicolour printing is your business, this is the one to get.

Ripple CEO Brad Garlinghouse Breaks Silence on XRP vs. RLUSD Debate

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Ripple CEO Brad Garlinghouse addressed the ongoing debate over the potential impact of Ripple’s RLUSD stablecoin on XRP’s future, asserting that both assets are complementary rather than competitive.

On Nov. 3, Brad Garlinghouse offered his view in response to a comprehensive X post from a notable member of the XRP Ledger community named Vet, who outlined the key differences between the two assets associated with Ripple.

“Well said, Vet. Complementary, not competitive,” the Ripple chief executive tweeted.

The brief statement comes amid pushback as some XRP holders are increasingly concerned that Ripple’s aggressive foray into the stablecoin market may negatively impact demand for XRP in the future.

As part of his viral post, Vet explained that the key difference between the two assets revolves around the purposes they serve on the XRP Ledger.

“RLUSD is optional. XRP isn’t,” the commentator explained.

As the XRP Ledger’s native currency, XRP is essential for the core functions of the network. First, every account must have an XRP reserve. Second, transaction fees are paid in XRP.

RLUSD, on the other hand, is a dollar-denominated stablecoin issued by Ripple's regulated stablecoin business. The key difference is that, unlike XRP, the value of RLUSD is supposed to remain stable at roughly $1.

Another key difference lies in control over the assets.

Ripple has the ability to freeze RLUSD under certain conditions, for example, to meet legal obligations. There is no central entity that can freeze XRP, the native cryptocurrency, on the ledger.

According to Vet, another key advantage of XRP is its privileged status on the XRP Ledger's DEX.

The XRP Ledger has an automatic bridging function. In this way, if it makes sense to trade between currencies through XRP for a better exchange rate, trades can be automatically routed through XRP.

This is why XRP is so valuable for liquidity providers and market makers as an intermediary asset.

On the other hand, RLUSD would be much more suitable in cases where price stability is important, for example, for payroll, invoicing, corporate payments, etc.

At the same time, some XRP investors are worried that one day RLUSD may effectively replace XRP for some payment use cases.

The crucial question is this: If you can send a dollar-denominated stablecoin directly, why would you need to use an intermediary like XRP?

It’s also worth noting that RLUSD isn’t exclusive to the XRP Ledger. In fact, RLUSD also works on Ethereum and other supported blockchains. So increasing demand for RLUSD doesn’t necessarily lead to more use of the XRP Ledger.

And even if we’re seeing more RLUSD transactions on the XRP Ledger, this doesn’t automatically mean that increased demand for XRP to pay transaction fees necessarily guarantees a significant price surge for the cryptocurrency.

It’s not the first time Garlinghouse has been forced to comment on Ripple's changing priorities.

During Ripple’s Swell 2026 conference in New York on Oct. 28, Ripple’s CEO directly addressed rumors that Ripple is becoming increasingly focused on RLUSD rather than XRP.

“We will never give up on XRP,” Garlinghouse said.

We Tried The World-Famous Apple Pie In A Bag—Is It Worth The Hype?

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Delish editors handpick every product we feature. We may earn commission from the links on this page.

Thanksgiving is only a few weeks away, so it’s already time to start planning your holiday menu. And with so many labor-intensive savory dishes on my list, I prefer to outsource dessert to the pros. Grocery store pies are often average at best, but there’s one out there that might actually be better than homemade.

The Elegant Farmer is a family-owned business based in Mukwonago, Wisconsin. In addition to operating an orchard with a dozen apple varieties, they’re best known as a specialty food store—largely thanks to its award-winning Apple Pie in a Paper Bag.

Over the past several decades, the signature dessert has achieved national acclaim and developed a cult following. Gourmet and The Wall Street Journal have dubbed it the best apple pie in America, and The Elegant Farmer even bested Bobby Flay in a 2008 episode of Throwdown.

Now, dessert lovers nationwide can get one of these famous pies delivered through Goldbelly. However, a single pie costs a whopping $70, including shipping. But is the Apple Pie in a Paper Bag worth the hype—and the sticker shock? I got my hands on one to find out.

The bag isn’t just a marketing schtick. 26 years ago, The Elegant Farmer’s former owners, Dan and Karen Scheel, wanted to sell a new dessert using the apples grown in their orchard. Karen found a recipe in a cookbook for a pie baked in a paper bag, so she enlisted their team of bakers to develop their own version.

The paper bag offers two benefits. The sealed environment traps moisture as it bakes to keep the Ida Red apples juicy, but it also prevents the crust from burning. Partway through baking, The Elegant Farmer cuts a hole in the center of the bag so that the top crust is perfectly golden by the end.

When you order this pie, it arrives pre-baked and frozen in a shrink-wrapped cardboard box. All you need to do is warm it straight from frozen in a 325°F oven for about an hour. If you thaw it in the refrigerator first, you can reheat it at 425°F for just 8 to 10 minutes. Yes, it’s that easy.

The instructions direct you to place the pie on a baking tray, but I’d recommend going one step further and laying down a protective layer of parchment first. A considerable amount of caramelized sugar collects at the bottom of the bag and can leak into the oven. Adding the extra barrier is a simple step that spares you from scrubbing it off later.

An hour later, the pie was warm and ready to serve. The torn paper and cracked crust admittedly don’t make this the most visually stunning pie in the world. But once our editors took a bite, any aesthetic concerns went out the window.

Contributing Writer Steven Morea said the apples taste like “they were just picked.” Digital Director Katelyn Lunders appreciated the abundance of apples and how the filling wasn’t too sweet. “I can’t stop eating it,” she added. (Lunders is already planning on ordering another for her family Thanksgiving, by the way.)

The crust got equally rave reviews. “I would buy this just for the crust,” said Senior Editor Samantha MacAvoy. The pronounced butter flavor, golden caramelization, and crunchy texture perfectly offset the softness of the fruit. Even Digital Community Editor Taylor Worden—a self-proclaimed pie hater—fell in love with it. And Test Kitchen Manager Alejandro Valdes Lora kept his opinion short and sweet: “I’m obsessed with this.”

If our office’s unanimous praise is any indication, The Elegant Farmer’s Apple Pie in a Paper Bag is absolutely worth the hype. We may hesitate to spend $70 on this pie on any ordinary day. But for special occasions like Thanksgiving, it’s a convenient showstopper that’ll easily upstage everything else on your holiday menu.

Voting in space: NASA astronauts cast ballots from International Space Station

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Space.com's Sci-Fi Reader's Club. Read a sci-fi short story every month and join a virtual community of fellow science fiction fans!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The three Americans currently living off Earth just made sure their electoral voices were heard.

Anil Menon told us today (Nov. 3) that he and fellow NASA astronauts Luke Delaney and Jessica Watkins voted in the U.S. midterm elections from the International Space Station (ISS).

"Yes, you can even vote from space! All three U.S. crew members had the privilege of voting from space station. We filled out an electronic ballot and sent it securely to the election officials," Menon wrote in an X post this morning.

Yes, you can even vote from space! All three US crew members had the privilege of voting from space station. We filled out an electronic ballot and sent it securely to the election officials. https://t.co/hJCExQ2nKrNovember 3, 2026

That post included a 29-second video, in which Menon — wearing, like many of us today, an "I voted" sticker — shows us his "voting booth."

It's actually his room on the ISS, Menon explained. "Inside of it is my computer, where I was able to fill out an electronic form and send it to Harris County so I could vote today," he said.

Most NASA astronauts register to vote in Texas' Harris County; it's home to Johnson Space Center in Houston, the agency's human spaceflight hub.

Americans have been able to vote from space since 1997, when Texas passed a law allowing this to happen. The first person to take advantage of the legislation was NASA astronaut David Wolf, who cast his ballot from Russia's Mir space station that same year. (The ISS didn't welcome its first crew until November 2000.)

"Just like any other American away from home, astronauts may fill out a Federal Post Card Application to request an absentee ballot," NASA officials wrote in an astronaut-voting explainer.

The process is somewhat involved, however.

"After an astronaut fills out an electronic ballot aboard the orbiting laboratory, the document flows through NASA's Tracking and Data Relay Satellite System to a ground antenna at the agency's White Sands Test Facility in Las Cruces, New Mexico," they added. "From New Mexico, NASA transfers the ballot to the Mission Control Center at NASA Johnson and then on to the county clerk responsible for casting the ballot. To preserve the vote's integrity, the ballot is encrypted and accessible only by the astronaut and the clerk."

Delaney and Watkins launched to the ISS on Oct. 1 on SpaceX's Crew-13 mission. Menon has been there since mid-July, arriving aboard a Russian Soyuz spacecraft.

The trio currently share the orbiting lab with four other people — Canadian Space Agency astronaut Joshua Kutryk and cosmonauts Anna Kikina, Pyotr Dubrov and Sergey Teteryatnikov. Kutryk and Teteryatnikov are members of Crew-13, whereas Dubrov and Kikina shared the Soyuz with Menon.

Court seems poised to side with Catholic preschools in religious discrimination dispute

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

The Supreme Court on Tuesday appeared ready to hold that the exclusion of Catholic preschools in Colorado from the state’s universal preschool program violates the First Amendment. After just over two hours of oral argument in St. Mary Catholic Parish v. Roy, a majority seemed to agree with the challengers that Colorado’s refusal to provide them with an exemption from rules that would require the preschools to admit all students – including LGBTQ children and children with LGBTQ parents – should be subject to a more stringent constitutional test than that applied by the lower court. The court could decide to apply that standard itself or send the case back to the lower courts for them to do so.

Colorado launched the universal preschool program, which provides families in the state with up to 15 hours of free preschool per week at participating schools, in 2023. To participate in the program, preschools must meet a variety of state standards and sign a nondiscrimination agreement, in which they consent to “provide eligible children an equal opportunity to enroll and receive preschool services regardless of race, ethnicity, religious affiliation, sexual orientation, gender identity, lack of housing, income level, or disability.” The preschools cannot decline to serve LGBTQ families, because Colorado believes such refusals would violate the nondiscrimination agreement.

The plaintiffs – the Archdiocese of Denver, two Catholic parishes with preschools (one of which has since closed), and two parents of preschool-aged children – went to federal court to challenge the state’s policy. They contended that requiring Catholic preschools to agree to the nondiscrimination agreement to participate in the universal preschool program constitutes unlawful religious discrimination and that the First Amendment requires Colorado to carve out a religious exemption that allows them to take part in the program.

After both the district court and the U.S. Court of Appeals for the 10th Circuit rejected the challengers’ claim, they came to the Supreme Court last year, asking the justices to take up the case – which the court agreed in April 2026 to do.

Representing the challengers, Nicholas Reaves told the justices that the schools in the Archdiocese of Denver “exist to pass on the faith to the next generation.” “[T]his is not a zero-sum game,” he said, because “letting Catholic schools” participate in the universal preschool program “will create more seats for everyone.”

Representing the state, Colorado Deputy Solicitor General Helen Norton countered that “Colorado welcomes religious preschools to” the program and allows them to teach their religious beliefs. It simply asks them, she emphasized, to comply with the same rules as everyone else.

One of the major points of contention at Tuesday’s argument was the significance of the Supreme Court’s 2022 decision in Carson v. Makin, in which the court struck down a Maine law that allowed public funding to be used for private schools, but not for schools that provide religious instruction. The case was the third in a trilogy of decisions on the availability of public funding for religious institutions. In Carson, the majority applied strict scrutiny – the toughest constitutional test – after it concluded that the Maine law barred some private schools from receiving public funds “solely because they are religious.”

In response to a question from Justice Clarence Thomas about whether a ruling for the challengers would require the court to extend its ruling in Carson, Reaves responded that it would not. In Carson, he insisted, the court’s “focus … was to look at … what is happening to the religious believer?” The court in Carson, he said, “didn’t focus on the text of the law in Maine. It focused on whether schools are being excluded on account of their religious exercise.”

Chief Justice John Roberts pressed Reaves on that point, observing that the law at issue in Carson specifically discriminated against religious schools – which, Roberts suggested, is not the case with the Colorado program.

Reaves pushed back. He noted that the laws in other cases involving public benefits had been neutral, rather than discriminating against religious people or organizations – for example, the court’s 1963 decision in Sherbert v. Verner, reversing a ruling by the South Carolina Supreme Court that had rejected a constitutional challenge to the denial of employment benefits from a Seventh-day Adventist whose religion barred her from working on Saturday.

Roberts later questioned Norton on a related point, asking her to differentiate the Colorado program from the law at issue in Carson and telling her that the effect of the Maine law was “pretty much the same as the effect of the law here.” He also told Norton that the state was trying to “bury Sherbert” even though “nothing in our precedent has backed away from” it.

Two of the court’s Democratic appointees, however, echoed Roberts’ initial skepticism that Carson dictated the outcome of the challengers’ case. Justice Elena Kagan told Reaves that the trilogy of cases culminating in Carson involved laws that had excluded religious institutions and schools because of their religion. “[I]t was a nondiscrimination principle,” she emphasized, but “you’re asking us to” issue a decision that would go “far beyond” its earlier cases. In particular, she suggested, the challengers were seeking a ruling that religious people “need a special accommodation” because of their faith.

Reaves responded that an interpretation of the court’s decision in Carson that focused on whether the law at issue expressly discriminated against religion would be “very easy to circumvent.”

Kagan responded that this isn’t “a good case to raise the kind of specter of circumvention.” “[T]his is a case,” she continued, “of a state trying to enforce its own nondiscrimination principles.”

Justice Ketanji Brown Jackson appeared to agree. She characterized Carson as a case that prohibited governments from treating religious schools worse than it treated secular schools. That doctrine, she posited, was “pretty narrow” and made “perfect sense.” But, she told Reaves, “your schools want to discriminate by exclusion” when no other school participating in the universal preschool program does so.

Some of the court’s Republican appointees were more persuaded by the challengers’ argument – also advanced by Sarah Harris, the principal deputy solicitor general who argued on behalf of the Trump administration in support of the challengers – that Carson should apply even if the Colorado law does not specifically target religion.

Justice Neil Gorsuch, for example, asked “what would be left” of cases like Sherbert “if everything turned on express discrimination” against religion, while Justice Samuel Alito appeared dubious that the legislators who enacted the Colorado universal preschool law “would not understand what the effect of” the law’s requirements “would be.”

Similarly, Justice Amy Coney Barrett asked Norton why, if Colorado cannot expressly exclude religious schools from its program, it can “impose a condition” – the nondiscrimination requirement – that it knows “schools will be unable to agree to?” Barrett pointed to a New York Times op-ed written in the wake of Carson by law professor Aaron Tang, who she stated suggested that states counteract some of the effects of Carson by enacting nondiscrimination requirements.

Norton resisted any effort to link Colorado’s nondiscrimination requirement to an intent to target religious schools, stressing that there was no evidence of the legislature’s motives and (in response to a question from Jackson) that the requirement predated the inclusion of religious schools in the program.

The justices and advocates also debated, although less extensively than the significance of Carson, whether Colorado’s conditions were neutral and generally applied to every preschool, regardless of religion – the kind of government actions, the Supreme Court held in 1990 in Employment Division v. Smith, that are subject to a less stringent constitutional test, known as rational-basis review.

Reaves told the justices that the Colorado nondiscrimination requirement was not the kind of “across-the-board” prohibition that the court envisioned in Smith because the Colorado law contains a series of preferences to facilitate the matching of families to preschools, including a “catch-all” preference that allows preschools to request (and the state to grant) a preference not listed in the regulation. Moreover, he added, the law also creates exceptions to accommodate children with disabilities and children from low-income families.

For her part, Norton characterized the preferences as very different from the kind of exclusion that the challengers seek. Even if a school receives a preference, she stressed, it is still subject to the nondiscrimination requirement.

Justice Brett Kavanaugh appeared to approach the case from what he regarded as a practical angle, asking Norton why the state wouldn’t “seek the win/win?” With over 2,000 preschools participating in the Colorado program, he said, there should be “plenty of opportunities to respect the rights of the religious believers” and, at the same time, “the ability of LGBT parents” to find a school that is a good fit for their families. “Why,” he asked, “has this been provoked?”

Justice Sonia Sotomayor took a different tack, suggesting that a ruling for the challengers could have broader implications. A Catholic preschool, she told Reaves, could reject a child “merely” because his or her parents are LGBTQ. Would this, she queried, also allow a religious preschool to turn down Black children?

Reaves said that it would not. He pointed to a 1983 case in which the Supreme Court, applying strict scrutiny, upheld the Internal Revenue Service’s decision to revoke the tax-exempt status of a private university and private school with racially discriminatory admissions policies. The government in that case, he emphasized, indicated that its interest in prohibiting such policies was not merely “compelling” (the first factor under strict scrutiny) but “overriding.”

He told the justices that strict scrutiny should similarly apply to this case. “And then the government can come forward and say we have a really good reason for” excluding Catholic preschools from the program. But, he concluded, “we just don’t think they’ve done that in this case.”

By the end of the argument, Sotomayor seemed to leave little doubt about where she stood. “No matter what line of cases we follow,” she said, “there will be tremendous problems and a great cost to someone.”

A decision is expected sometime next year.

Amy Howe is the co-founder of SCOTUSblog and its primary reporter.

Mark Noble Set For New West Ham Role Alongside Scott Parker

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Scott Parker’s impending arrival at West Ham United could be accompanied by another interesting appointment, with Mark Noble reportedly set to join his former teammate’s coaching staff. According to The Guardian, Noble is expected to form part of Parker’s backroom team, although the precise role has yet to be officially confirmed.

It’s a development I find particularly encouraging. As Claret & Hugh has already reported, Parker is set to return to the club following the dismissal of Nuno Espírito Santo, bringing with him an impressive record of three promotions from the Championship.

But the prospect of Parker and Noble working together offers something else entirely. We could have a manager and a senior member of his coaching staff who completely understand West Ham’s identity, what the club means to its supporters and the importance of developing our own players.

For me, this would be a really shrewd move, both politically and in terms of developing West Ham’s younger players. Noble has already been closely involved with the club’s footballing operations as sporting director, giving him valuable knowledge of the youngsters coming through the academy.

There has recently been discussion within West Ham’s recruitment department about improving the pathway between the academy and the first team. Having Noble directly involved in the coaching set-up could be an excellent way of putting that ambition into practice, particularly given his familiarity with the players and their development.

I’d imagine the likes of Lewis Orford, Ollie Scarles and Golambeckis would welcome the prospect of somebody who knows them so well being involved in first-team decisions. That doesn’t guarantee any of them a place, of course, but it should at least help ensure their abilities are properly understood and considered.

There is also something reassuring about the prospect of two former Hammers working together at a time when the club desperately needs a change of direction. Following Nuno’s dismissal, West Ham need to restore confidence and establish a clearer identity on the pitch.

Of course, knowing the club isn’t enough on its own. Parker and Noble will ultimately be judged on results, and the immediate priority remains getting West Ham promoted back to the Premier League.

Nevertheless, I think this would be an excellent appointment. Parker brings his Championship experience, Noble brings his knowledge of West Ham and its young players, and together they could provide exactly the sort of connection between the first team and academy that we’ve been missing.

There’s still the question of Noble’s precise job title and how his sporting director responsibilities would change, but the prospect of the pair working together is one I find genuinely exciting.

Delta SkyMiles® Reserve American Express Card

Earn 2 Delta Comfort Flight Certificates + 50K Miles

The Business Platinum Card® from American Express

As High As 300,000 points. Find Out Your Offer.

Gone are the days of the “sure thing” complimentary upgrade. But, for those who find themselves on the road more than once a month, top-tier elite status can mean the difference between being there for that special family event or a disappointing night in an airport hotel.

Due to a ton of work travel, I currently hold top-tier status in the Big Three US airline elite programs. As a Northern Virginia resident, United is my primary airline, and I earned its invitation-only Global Services status again this year. As I was trying to figure out which of these three status levels was the most rewarding, I realized I may not even have the best elite status in North America.

No, I’m not talking about Southwest Airlines A-List Preferred status. I think there’s a reasonable argument for looking north.

Air Canada Aeroplan Super Elite status may actually be better for many frequent travelers, even if it seems complicated as a U.S. resident.

Here are a few reasons to consider earning Super Elite status.

One area where Aeroplan really shines is lounge access — a perk that even top-tier elites at most U.S. airlines can't count on when flying domestically.

Super Elite members can bring immediate family into any Maple Leaf Lounge as well as an additional guest. Delta Diamond, United 1K and American Executive Platinum members get no complimentary lounge access as a benefit of their status.

While Aeroplan doesn’t grant access to their Signature Suites lounges, Super Elites can enjoy lounge access with partner airlines such as Emirates and Virgin Australia. They also have access to an arrivals lounge at London Heathrow.

Arguably one of the most valuable benefits of Super Elite status is Priority Rewards. With few exceptions, a Priority Reward can be redeemed for a 50% discount on a one-way or round-trip flight, including on partner airlines (and Air Canada has a lot of partners).

Aeroplan members can earn these rewards multiple times each year as part of Milestone Benefits.

If you hold 25K status, your Priority Rewards are valid on economy flights in Canada and the United States (including Hawaii). However, Super Elites can redeem them for all cabins up to and including business class, including on partner airlines.

When you consider the pricing of international business class award flights, it’s not hard to imagine saving 100,000 Aeroplan points or more when redeeming a Priority Reward on a round-trip ticket.

You can book a flight for someone else, even if they’re not immediate family, so long as you use your Aeroplan points.

If you travel as much as I do, you might consider the Air Canada Concierge Service to rival Priority Rewards in value. Super Elite members get access to a personalized concierge service.

Reports from Super Elite members indicate that Air Canada concierges have a healthy amount of autonomy to fix problems while you’re traveling, including moving you to other airlines to get you to your destination as quickly as possible. In many major airports, Air Canada has local concierges, so the support is right where you need it.

This level of service used to be commonplace with the U.S. carriers. While invitation-only levels such as United Global Services offer more flexible rules during irregular operations, my personal experience over the past few years is that many tools that used to be available to those agents have been restricted or removed.

How does a U.S. resident earn Super Elite status? It’s a bit complicated, but you have options. Cabotage restrictions prevent an airline like Air Canada from flying you between New York and Los Angeles, even if you connect in a Canadian gateway.

However, if you travel internationally, especially in a premium cabin, Air Canada is well situated to get you to many places in Europe and Asia. With hubs in Toronto, Montreal and Vancouver, AIr Canada can connect you to Europe and Asia. It may be easy to forget Toronto as an option if you live in Ohio or Indiana and need to fly to France.

The Chase Air Canada Aeroplan® Credit Card (see ) is also a great way to accelerate your quest for Super Elite status. Just holding the card gives you 5,000 Status Qualifying Credits each calendar year and entry-level 25K elite status. Spending on the card can earn another 20,000 Status Qualifying Credits. That means the credit card alone can get you 20% of the way to Super Elite.

Top-tier elite status is largely similar among American, Delta and United. While Air Canada shares many of the same benefits with them, Aeroplan has a few key benefits that set Super Elite status apart.

Priority Rewards allow your mileage balance to punch above its weight. Lounge access and a dedicated concierge service can make travel much smoother, even on the toughest days. And a few business class tickets to Europe can quickly put you in the range of Aeroplan Super Elite status.

The Indian Pavilion in Venice Asks: What Is Home?

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

"Geographies of Distance" offers a polysemous reading of home during a period of accelerated urbanization in India.

VENICE — The Indian Pavilion at the 61st Venice Biennale is to visual art what minor tones are to music: striking notes that invite contemplation and convey depth. Geographies of Distance: Remembering Home interprets the Biennale’s theme, “In Minor Keys,” through the rich diversity encompassed by India's name. The exhibition offers a polysemous reading of home in a country where time and space are measured on a vast scale, bringing together disparate experiences of place, memory, distance, and belonging.

The exhibition represents all of India: from the sun-baked earth of the southern state of Tamil Nadu to the stepped architecture of Ladakh in the north, to houses at the foot of the Himalayas, and to the ghost of a Delhi long succumbing to bulldozers. Enormous flowers hang from the ceiling, standing in for gardens and their most distinctive inhabitants, a tribute to their prominent place in Indian private, public, and sacred life. A mesh of bamboo canes evokes a widespread construction technique in the East, symbolizing new cities rising above older urban landscapes.

“I would characterize the pavilion and the work of the five artists as a reflection on identity and belonging in a period of accelerated change,” said Amin Jaffer, curator of Geographies of Distance. “[It] proposes the idea that home is not solely a physical place we inhabit but a condition that we carry with us.”
