Amazon rewrites its software engineering playbook for an era when AI writes the code

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

by Todd Bishop on Nov 3, 2026 at 12:26 pmNovember 3, 2026 at 12:36 pm

Last month, a small team inside Amazon Web Services called Velocity Labs went from concept to the public release of a new open-source AI model in less than a week.

The group, whose existence hasn’t been previously reported, was formed in April. Velocity Labs has about 35 people, most of them in Seattle. The idea is to think beyond Amazon’s normal product-oriented approach, building quick prototypes in areas of AI that the company sees as underexplored and increasingly important to customers.

“We have noticed how the open-source community, and some of our competitors, experiment with new ideas, and deliver initial products fast,” the company says in job listings. “With Velocity Labs we are building an organization that can move faster, explore more, and make sure AWS is at the cutting edge of innovation in agentic AI.”

Inside the group, scientists and engineers work together, and they’re encouraged to cross over: engineers engage in research, and scientists do engineering work.

Velocity Labs is an example of the ways Amazon is trying “to create organizations that we think are going to be better suited to working with this new set of tools,” said Marc Brooker, an AWS vice president and distinguished engineer who leads the team.

For the vast majority of Amazon’s developers, he said, AI agents now write almost all the code that would have previously been written by hand.

Redefining the role: The team is part of Amazon’s larger rethinking of software development for the AI era, laid out in revised guidelines that the company’s engineering leaders sent to its developers earlier this year.

A letter that accompanied the guidelines, published last week under the byline of Michael “Terk” Terkowitz, a VP and distinguished engineer in Amazon’s stores business, advises engineers to learn AI now and to stay close to their customers and products.

The letter acknowledges that as Amazon has grown, some engineers and managers have become “less connected to their customers and business.”

Amazon has never published its underlying role guidelines for software engineers, and it declined to provide a copy of the revised version for this article. Brooker, one of the authors of the accompanying letter, said in an interview that the update emphasizes measuring engineers and teams by outcomes rather than inputs.

“We had seen these anti-patterns in the industry of folks really focusing on how many tokens are you spending, how many lines of code are you committing,” Brooker said, explaining that those kinds of measures don’t equate to customer value.

Asked whether AI use is part of performance reviews or promotions, Brooker said, “Not directly in a metrics sense.” It could factor in, he said, to the extent that it makes someone a stronger performer.

The letter doesn’t dismiss usage metrics entirely. Amazon has found that they “correlate well with the outputs that matter” and expects that to continue “for some time,” it says.

But what’s important, the letter says, is to use them “with care and judgement.”

Amazon engineers have described pressure to use AI tools. Three developers told The New York Times last year that managers were pushing them to use AI, raising output goals and becoming less forgiving about deadlines, with some coders saying their jobs had begun to resemble warehouse work. Amazon has set a goal for more than 80% of its developers to use AI each week, the Financial Times reported.

In May, Amazon shut down an internal leaderboard called KiroRank — which ranked employees by how much they used Kiro, the company’s AI coding tool — after finding that some were using the tool excessively just to climb the rankings, the FT reported.

Asked whether that episode was part of the impetus for the revised guidelines, Brooker said he didn’t recall the leaderboard or the details surrounding it.

“If your work does not benefit from current AI tooling, that is a finding, not a failing. Document why, share it with your community of peers, bring it to your manager, and work to find solutions.”

Letter accompanying the revised SDE role guidelines

How the work has changed: Two years ago, most Amazon developers were still writing their code by hand, Brooker said. Today, “for the vast majority of our developer population, almost all of their implementation code is agent generated rather than human written,” he said.

“That is a massive change in the way of working for software engineers,” he said.

Brooker said he used to spend much of his time in a traditional code editor or the command line, and now spends a lot more of it working with agent tools such as Kiro, Kiro Crew and Anthropic’s Claude Code, calling it “a complete change of tools.”

For the company, the transition hasn’t been seamless. In March, Amazon acknowledged that an engineer who followed inaccurate advice from an AI agent contributed to one of several outages on its retail site, though the company disputed a Financial Times report that junior and mid-level engineers would need senior engineers to sign off on AI-assisted changes.

Amazon also pushed back on an earlier FT report that a 13-hour disruption to an AWS service in December happened after engineers let its Kiro AI coding tool make changes, and the tool decided to “delete and recreate the environment.” Amazon blamed user error — a misconfigured role — and said it has since added safeguards, including mandatory peer review for production access.

Still, the letter acknowledges that today’s AI tools won’t help in every situation, and it says that’s OK as long as engineers can explain why.

“If your work does not benefit from current AI tooling, that is a finding, not a failing,” the letter says. “An engineer who can explain where AI does not yet help their work is demonstrating the judgement we are asking for.”

Inside Velocity Labs: The group is part of the agentic AI division led by Swami Sivasubramanian, which has organized much of its work around small teams of six to eight people, as GeekWire reported in June. Velocity Labs also includes the developers behind Strands, AWS’s open-source toolkit for building AI agents.

The model that Velocity Labs released last month, Strands Decider 2B, is designed to make quick, narrow decisions inside AI agents, such as which tool to use or where to route a request. It’s based on Qwen, an open model from Alibaba, and was released Oct. 1 along with the recipe used to train it.

Brooker built the original version on his own GPU at home after the startup TypeSafe AI unveiled Jev, a similar decision model, on Sept. 15, he wrote on his blog. “Every line of code was written by an agent,” he wrote, though he tried to make sure “the core ideas and insights were mine, or at least I understood them.”

Brooker described the team’s turnaround as less than a week from concept to public release. OpenAI announced a similar hosted service, its Decisions API, the same week, TechCrunch reported.

Brooker credited the team’s speed to its small size and a high level of trust: each person takes ownership of a piece of a project and asks for help when needed. He expects Amazon to create more teams like it, though not across the whole company.

Impact on jobs: Amazon has cut roughly 30,000 corporate jobs since late 2025. CEO Andy Jassy has said he expects AI to shrink the company’s corporate workforce over time.

At the same time, AWS CEO Matt Garman said in April that Amazon is “hiring just as many software developers as we ever had,” Business Insider reported. An Amazon spokesperson told the publication that more than 11,000 interns and early-career software engineers would join the company globally this year.

Asked whether AI will mean fewer engineers at Amazon, Brooker said, “I don’t think it changes the number of engineers.” The company wants engineers who take ownership, are curious and can learn quickly outside their areas of expertise, he said.

AI isn’t the right answer for every problem, Brooker said.

If the right tool is “an AI agent powered by a cutting-edge model, fantastic,” he said. However, he added, if it’s “three lines of Python code, that’s great too.”

In just a few weeks, AWS re:Invent brings advanced and expert-level sessions on the services you build on every day, from serverless and containers to databases and developer tools. Go hands-on with new launches the same week they’re announced, and hear directly from the engineers who built them. Experience the depth you won’t find anywhere else.

This DC Designer’s Nearly 200-Year-Old Virginia Farmhouse Flourishes With Detail and History

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

In the foothills of Virginia’s Blue Ridge Mountains, just minutes from Shenandoah National Park, the town of Sperryville (population: 350) still feels like a well-kept secret. In recent years, it’s attracted the eye of Washington, DC–based designer Melissa Colgan of Melissa Colgan Interiors, who purchased a weekend home there in 2023.

Though born in the Midwest, the designer and her husband, Daniel Schned, moved to DC in 2016 and spent their early years and weekends exploring the surrounding countryside. “One of the areas we fell in love with is Rappahannock County,” she says of discovering its scenic pastures, rolling hills, and grazing cows. “It feels a world away from DC.”

The staircase is covered in Schumacher’s Caterpillar Leaf print and the stair runner is by Sinclair Till. The cloche is custom by Dan Nakamura.

In the family room, the BDDW sofa is upholstered in a Décors Barbares fabric. The side table is from Covesville Antiques and topped with a lamp by Christopher Spitzmiller. The artwork is by Molly van Amerongen.

The fruitwood dining table from 1stDibs is surrounded with chairs by Lorenzo Castillo and upholstered in a fabric by Schumacher. The 1930s chandelier has custom shades covered in a Decors Barbares fabric. The chairs are by Leah O’Connell (left) and Soane. The artwork is by Joshua Perkin.

In 2020, the couple eloped at The Inn at Little Washington and began looking for a vacation home in the area. Three years later, they found a listing for an 1830s farmhouse and barn, which happened to be the former home and office of the county doctor. “It’s a very special house in Sperryville because it’s where the doctor saw all his patients in the early 1900s,” Colgan says.

What began as an 1830s single-room log cabin evolved to include a barn and second story in the 1890s, followed by another addition in the late 1990s. “A lot of the original details were still there,” Colgan says of the 1,200-square-foot residence. “We just wanted to freshen things up a bit.”

In the family living room, the windows, along with the original chink log siding, date to the 1830s. “I wanted to lean into some of the depth that was already there,” Colgan says of her color palette. “That prompted me into the world of deep reds, ochres, and blues.”

In a cozy corner, a Casamidy chair covered in soft mohair flanks a Swedish Gustavian oak secretary from the 1800s.

Colgan solicited the help of DC architect Alexander Smith of Mortar & Thatch. “The thing we aligned with from the very beginning is we didn't want it to feel like a new renovation,” Smith says. “Maybe we slapped on some paint or got new countertops, but we didn’t want it to seem like we were implanting foreign ideas.”

The biggest task was subdividing the single bedroom upstairs to accommodate Colgan’s growing family. At the time, she was pregnant with her now two-year-old daughter. “We basically took down the walls of the one bedroom, made a middle bedroom for our daughter, then eked every inch we could in order to fit a king bed and built-in closets in our room,” she says. The result is a quaint and colorful two-bedroom blueprint, with dappled light dancing through the street-facing windows.

In the pocket-size half bath, an American Federal mahogany mirror broadens the space, which is wrapped in Iksel Mogul Berry Wallpaper by Schumacher and adorned with an oil painting by Jay Harper.

Incorporating open shelves in the kitchen helped maximize storage while enabling the designer to showcase more of the home’s attractive chink log siding. Farrow & Ball’s Terre de Egypte paint accentuates Colgan’s collections of splatterware mugs and vintage objects, like an early-20th-century painter’s palette.

Today, they spend most of their time in the living room downstairs, with its original chink log siding from the 1830s. “The room is so soulful. You just walk in and take a deep breath,” she says. “The thing that came to mind early on was a little Russian country house or dacha. When we first saw the listing, we were in Switzerland, so that was the original seed for the design.”

Drawing from the warmth and depth of the living room’s original pine floors and log siding, Colgan conjured a rich color palette of ochres, reds, and blues with botanical and floral-patterned fabrics that mirrored the pastoral landscape. “We’re surrounded by mountains and flora and fauna, so I wanted the house to reflect that as well.”

A brisbee curtain in Lake August’s flowering Nasturtium fabric adds light and whimsy to the kitchen’s neutral cabinetry.

The kitchen pendants are by Urban Electric. The walls are painted in Dimity and the cabinets are painted Joa’s White, both by Farrow & Ball. The rug is by Matt Camron.

The furnishings are an array of old, new, and local finds from their 1920s French fruitwood dining table to the early American rocking chair salvaged from Ruckersville Antiques. “We didn’t really have anything for this house because the style is so different from our city home, so we had to start from scratch,” Colgan says. Still, the designer is an avid collector and had some pieces sitting in storage like the BDDW sofa, purchased at auction, that’s now in her living room. Currently, it’s upholstered in Décors Barbares’ lively Roses Tchékhonine print, with winged butterflies flitting across a canvas of cobalt and crimson flowers.

In the primary bedroom, every inch mattered. “We did multiple layouts and wanted to make sure the details, finishes, and everything in there was an appropriate scale so things didn’t feel too big or too small for the space,” says Smith. The walls and his-and-her closets are painted in Farrow & Ball’s Pink Ground and Eating Room Red, with Roman shades done in a Lucrezia fabric by Fortuny. An acrylic and ink work on paper by artist Julie Wolfe, which Colgan gave to her husband for their first wedding anniversary, hangs over the bed.

In the sunlit bathroom, blue and white garden tiles from Marble Systems bloom alongside fittings by Waterworks, D Porthault patterned towels, and an antique French mirror, circa 1785.

Other spaces, like the mudroom and laundry located near the home’s side entrance, were made more welcoming and useful, with cupboards dressed in wholesome, hay-colored gingham skirts. Reconfiguring appliances and adding cabinets and open shelving helped optimize the kitchen’s storage and traffic flow. “We were able to inch out so much in such a small space,” Smith says.

Despite its demure size, the house today is a sensory retreat for the couple. “That first Thanksgiving was a real stress test,” Colgan says. The barn in the back, where the doctor kept his horse for making house calls across the county, was renovated by the previous owner to include a kitchenette, an open living room space, and sleeping quarters. “The barn had pigs in it up until the last owner, and he renovated it,” she says. “I’ve since layered in a lot of fabrics and antiques.”

In their daughter’s room, a late-19th-century faux-bamboo pine and bird’s eye maple bed is made up with D Porthault Pink Coeur’s linens and a one-of-a-kind PROJEKTITYYNY Wisteria baby quilt, stitched with Liberty fabrics. The bookshelf is from Crate & Barrel and the walls and windows are covered in Ottoline’s blue and brown Indian Tulip print.

The walls are covered in Caterpillar Leaf by Schumacher and the slipper chair is custom. The Roman shade is a fabric by Antoinette Poisson.

Off the home’s side entry, a laundry and mudroom papered in Beata Heuman’s Eyelash design is further brightened thanks to the café curtain and cabinet skirts in a jumbo gingham by Beata Heuman, finished with French grosgrain trim.

Ultimately, however, it was the historic farmhouse where Colgan concentrated her creative energy. “A lot of times people come into these old houses and instead of trying to think carefully about how to make modern life function there, they blow out walls and make everything open,” she says. Instead, she champions her home’s nuanced details, including planks of weathered wood that have patinated over time or the way sunlight shimmers off the 19th-century windows, with their panes of wavy glass. “They are very much not energy efficient,” she says, “but they are absolutely beautiful.”

The custom console is painted in Benjamin Moore’s Van Deusen Blue and the walls are covered in Beata Heuman’s Eyelash Spring. The 1960s mirror is from 1stDibs.

On the front porch, a five-piece set by Newell Jacobsen is upholstered in a sky-blue Lisa Fine Madras Performance fabric that complements the painted ceiling, and the neighbor’s vibrant home. “There are some fun houses on our street—the one across the street is yellow,” Colgan says. “I liked the idea of colors out there being a little brighter.”

Why Airbnb Is Spending Big on LA Elections

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Enter your email for a complimentary article + exclusive offers.

Photo Credit: Cars parked near the Hollywood sign, one of Los Angeles' most famous landmarks. Pexels / Daniel Semenov

Airbnb’s LA election spending is framed around the 2028 Olympics. A bigger bet could be on the rules after the Games.

Few companies have spent more on Los Angeles’ elections this year than Airbnb, which has put millions behind Mayor Karen Bass and other local candidates.

Airbnb’s spending touches several fights that matter to its business in the city, including a proposed temporary easing of short-term rental restrictions during the 2028 Olympics, which are expected to draw in roughly 2 million visitors to the greater Los Angeles area. As much as $4.3 billion in direct visitor spending is expected to benefit hotels, restaurants, and retailers, according to a study from the Los Angeles County Economic Development Corporation commissioned by LA28.

Airbnb is looking for a bigger slice of that spending.

Since 2018, only primary residences have been allowed to list on platforms like Airbnb. Proposals for a vacation rental expansion would ease restrictions through the end of 2028.

A restriction rollback — even a temporary one — could help Airbn

Airbnb has spent more than $12 million on Los Angeles politics since 2025, backing Mayor Karen Bass, city attorney candidate John McKinney, and two City Council candidates, while pushing a Vacation Rental Ordinance that would temporarily let second homes be listed through the end of 2028. Los Angeles has limited short-term rentals to primary residences since 2018, and the 2028 Olympics — expected to draw roughly 2 million visitors and $4.3 billion in direct visitor spending, according to a study commissioned by LA28 — is the opening Airbnb is using to argue the city needs more lodging. The City Council struck the vacation rental program from the budget it adopted in May, and the planning department's August framework would sunset eased rules in December 2028, limit permits to individual owners and trusts, and cap them at about 15,000 units — a cap planning says the city has too few second homes to reach. City finance staff estimated the program might generate $25 million to $30 million in tourist tax, well below the up to $110 million cited by Airbnb's coalition. Los Angeles hoteliers, including the general manager of the Hoxton Downtown LA, told the Council the city's 113,750 county hotel rooms are already enough for the Olympics, World Cup, and Super Bowl.

Omega-3 DHA Eases Autism Behaviors by Calming Inflammation

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

A preclinical study from Chongqing Medical University and Guangzhou Women and Children’s Medical Center demonstrates that docosahexaenoic acid (DHA), a crucial omega-3 polyunsaturated fatty acid, alleviates autism-like behavioral deficits in young rats exposed to early-life maternal separation stress.

Publishing in Pediatric Discovery, researchers revealed that daily DHA supplementation restored social interaction, curtailed repetitive self-grooming, and reduced anxiety. Molecular and network pharmacology analyses showed that DHA directly targets and inhibits the pro-inflammatory c-Jun N-terminal kinase (JNK) pathway while activating NRF2 antioxidant defenses within the prefrontal cortex, offering mechanistic insights into nutritional neuroscience approaches for neurodevelopmental conditions.

Source: Children’s Hospital of Chongqing Medical University / Guangzhou Women and Children’s Medical Center

Autism spectrum disorder (ASD) encompasses a heterogeneous continuum of early neurodevelopmental phenotypes characterized by persistent challenges in social communication, atypical sensory processing, and restricted, repetitive behavioral patterns. While the fundamental etiology involves complex polygenic and epigenetic architectures, mounting clinical evidence indicates that systemic immune dysregulation, neuroinflammation, and redox imbalances are common pathophysiological features in many autistic children.

Elevated circulating cytokines and depleted cellular antioxidant buffers create a neurotoxic cellular environment, frequently impairing neural plasticity and synaptic transmission within the prefrontal cortex (PFC), the cerebral locus governing executive control, social cognition, and repetitive impulse inhibition.

Docosahexaenoic acid (DHA), an essential long-chain omega-3 polyunsaturated fatty acid highly enriched in cerebral grey matter, is fundamental to synaptic membrane fluidity, dendritic arborization, and myelin sheath maintenance. Although dietary omega-3 interventions have shown mixed, exploratory benefits in small-scale human trials, the precise molecular mechanisms by which DHA could modify autism-related neural signaling remained elusive.

Now, a multi-method investigation published in Pediatric Discovery provides direct evidence that DHA mitigates autism-like behavioral phenotypes by dampening the hyperactive c-Jun N-terminal kinase (JNK) stress-signaling cascade and restoring antioxidant defenses in the brain.

The researchers, based at the Children’s Hospital of Chongqing Medical University and Guangzhou Women and Children’s Medical Center, evaluated the therapeutic potential of DHA using the neonatal maternal separation (NMS) rat model. In this established neurodevelopmental paradigm, rodent pups undergo three hours of maternal separation daily from postnatal day 1 through 21, followed by individual isolation housing until day 56—an early environmental trauma that reproduces core autism-like behaviors.

Rats administered daily DHA (80 mg/kg/day) demonstrated marked behavioral improvements between postnatal days 42 and 56:

To map the biochemical signaling networks mediating these behavioral recoveries, the team integrated network pharmacology with molecular docking simulations, Western blotting, and enzyme-linked immunosorbent assays (ELISA).

Network analysis revealed 13 core overlapping genetic targets between DHA bioactivity and ASD pathology, notably involving genes encoding tumor necrosis factor (TNF), interleukin-6 (IL6), superoxide dismutase 1 (SOD1), catalase (CAT), brain-derived neurotrophic factor (BDNF), and peroxisome proliferator-activated receptor alpha (PPARA). Computational docking simulations predicted strong direct binding between DHA and key mediators, including JNK, CAT, SOD1, TNF-α, IL-6, and heme oxygenase-1 (HO-1), with binding affinities ranging from −2.93 to −6.02 kcal/mol.

Biochemical analysis of prefrontal cortex tissue validated these computational predictions:

The authors emphasize that while these findings outline a clear molecular framework connecting omega-3 supplementation to reduced neuroinflammation and improved social behavior, the results remain strictly preclinical.

Because the study utilized a neonatal stress-induced animal model, the results cannot be directly extrapolated to human clinical cohorts, nor do they suggest that omega-3 fatty acids can replace established clinical interventions, speech therapies, or behavioral supports.

Future translational research will require carefully stratified clinical trials with rigorous behavioral metrics and biomarker validation to determine whether specific neurodevelopmental subgroups—particularly individuals with verified elevations in baseline oxidative stress or cytokine dysregulation—can experience clinical gains from targeted adjunctive DHA therapies.

National Natural Science Foundation of China (82071395, 82101466, 32371030); Chongqing Medical University Program for Youth Innovation in Future Medicine (W0044); Doctoral Initiation Fund of Women and Children’s Medical Center of Guangzhou Medical University (1600037-04); Science and Technology Program of Guangzhou (2024A03J1244).

Docosahexaenoic acid ameliorates autistic-like behaviors by inhibiting oxidative stress and inflammatory response in neonatal maternal separation rats

Autism spectrum disorder (ASD) is a pervasive neurodevelopmental disorder characterized by impaired social interactions and communication, repetitive, or stereotyped behavior. Docosahexaenoic acid (DHA), an essential polyunsaturated fatty acid, has been demonstrated to exert anti-oxidative stress and anti-inflammatory properties, while also promoting myelin development and neural differentiation and development. However, it remains uncertain whether DHA can ameliorate autistic-like behaviors, and if so, the underlying mechanisms are still unclear.

Here, we established a neonatal maternal separation (NMS) rat model and treated it with DHA (80 mg/kg/day, i.p.). The results showed DHA treatment significantly alleviated autism-like behaviors in the NMS rats during their juvenile period. Subsequently, we employed network pharmacology analysis and molecular docking methods to screen potential targets of DHA in ASD therapy.

Through Gene Ontology, Kyoto Encyclopedia of Genes and Genomes, and Wikipedia enrichment analysis, we identified anti-oxidative stress, anti-inflammatory and JNK signaling pathway that might be associated with DHA-mediated improvement of autistic-like behaviors.

Furthermore, western blotting assays showed DHA significantly downregulated the expression levels of p-JNK and c-JUN, while upregulating the expression levels of NRF2, HO-1, SOD1, and CAT. In addition, enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay results showed DHA effectively reduced the production of pro-inflammatory factors, such as TNF-α, IL-6, and IL-1β.

Collectively, our study predicted and validated that DHA exhibits the potential to improve autistic-like behaviors induced by NMS in rats by suppressing JNK activation and inhibiting oxidative stress and inflammatory response. These findings suggest that DHA may be a potential therapeutic agent for the treatment of ASD.

Compass International Holdings Announces Merger With Rate to Combine Mortgage Ventures

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

In another big move for the company, Compass International Holdings has announced a merger with fintech company Rate to unite the two companies’ mortgage ventures—Guaranteed Rate Affinity and OriginPoint—under the OriginPoint brand, becoming a top 10 national mortgage lender, according to a release from the two companies

Guaranteed Rate Affinity was founded in partnership with Anywhere Real Estate back in 2017, making the merger an unsurprising move due to Compass’s acquisition of Anywhere last year.

Robert Reffkin, chairman and CEO of Compass International Holdings, said in a release that by bringing the two joint ventures together, “we can now serve our clients better than ever before.”

“We can connect more great real estate professionals with more great loan officers to create a simpler, more seamless and connected experience for homebuyers,” he continued.

The merger expands OriginPoint’s resources and geographic reach, the companies noted in a release, while backing the business with Rate’s AI technology, products and lending platform. The newly expanded OriginPoint is also connected “seamlessly” to Compass International Holdings’ owned-brokerage network of more than 80,000 real estate professionals, the largest in the country.

OriginPoint was originally founded in partnership with Rate, which also assisted in founding @properties’ Proper Rate—now under the Compass brand post acquisition of Christie’s International Real Estate.

Victor Ciardelli, founder and CEO of Rate, said in a release that this merger “creates a powerhouse lending platform never before seen in the real estate industry,” citing over “200,000 buy-side purchase transactions representing more than $200 billion in purchase volume” between Coldwell Banker Realty and Compass alone.

“OriginPoint now connects the best real estate professionals and the best loan officers in the country,” he continued. “It is a once-in-a-generation opportunity for best-in-class loan officers to exponentially grow their businesses.”

Compass noted that it will continue to invest in building “the most efficient platform,” and that OriginPoint customers will receive a streamlined real estate experience, allowing them to find, secure and close on a home in as few as 10 days with a more connected, personalized experience featuring greater choice and support.

Ciardelli added that this merger is “just the beginning of what’s to come.”

“This combination brings our best-in-class team, technology, service and leadership together to create a tremendous opportunity for loan officers to take full advantage of the $200 billion in buy-side transactions available to them to win more deals and help more consumers,” he continued.

Ciardelli also said that along with giving more opportunities to Rate’s loan officers to build their business, “we’re going to recruit like crazy with plans to strategically onboard hundreds of high-performing mortgage originators across the country to serve the increased volume demands from our 80,000+ real estate partners.”

I Took Olive Oil Shots Every Morning For A Month—Is The Health Hype Real?

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Delish editors handpick every product we feature. We may earn commission from the links on this page.

I’ll try just about anything that promises to make me look and feel better, but drinking olive oil? That one gave me pause—until I read up on the impressive benefits and found out Hailey Bieber does it every day. Suddenly, I was down to ditch my green juice habit to sip this alleged liquid gold.

But is there any real advantage to drinking it straight versus incorporating it into your regular diet? Or is it just another TikTok fad that sounds better on your FYP than it works IRL? I decided to take pre-made olive oil shots for an entire month to put the hype to the test. Here’s what happened.

If you didn’t already know, extra-virgin olive oil is a nutritional powerhouse! It’s rich in monounsaturated fats and antioxidants, and research has linked consumption to lower LDL (aka the “bad” cholesterol), better cardiovascular health, and reduced inflammation. It may also support healthy blood sugar levels and brain health, which is pretty cool considering it’s an ingredient we all probably have in our kitchen cabinets.

“Olive oil also contains polyphenols, which are natural plant compounds that help protect our cells from oxidative stress and support a healthy inflammatory response,” Maura MacDonald, M.S., R.D., C.S.S.D., registered dietitian at Thorne, tells Delish. “These compounds can benefit gut health too by interacting with the bacteria in our digestive system and helping to support microbial diversity and a healthy gut microbiome. It’s one of the reasons olive oil is such a staple of the Mediterranean diet, which has long been associated with better overall health and longevity.”

Listen, I knew that chugging olive oil wasn’t going to be a pleasant experience, but I wasn’t expecting it to be quite so, ahem, interesting. My main gripe was with the consistency: It was just a little too thick and viscous to go down easily, and every shot required a full pep talk.

I didn’t notice much difference after day 5, which left me feeling discouraged. I had hoped for an immediate click—glowing skin, boosted energy levels, less bloating, something!—but the only tangible evidence of my participation in the experiment was a glue-like coating that clung to my tonsils after every morning shot.

By week two, I called an audible and started adding lemon juice to the olive oil. Anything to cut the oiliness just a little—plus, the citrus brings its own nutritional perks! And while it wasn’t a perfect fix, it made the process a little more bearable.

Midway through the month, I started to notice improvements. My skin seemed more hydrated, my digestion was more regular, and I wasn’t dealing with the same post-meal bloating I’ve dealt with for years. I also found myself filling up faster, and I didn’t feel the same pull to go back for seconds. Whether it was the olive oil or a placebo effect, I’m not entirely sure, but at least I finally felt like the morning routine was doing something.

As the experiment progressed, I noticed my energy levels improve. I wasn’t exactly jumping out of bed like a morning person (let’s not get carried away here), but I was able to avoid the usual afternoon slump. I had fewer cravings too and wasn’t automatically reaching for snacks between mealtimes. My body just felt like it was functioning better.

I’m so glad you asked! Yes, yes, it does. According to MacDonald, it actually makes a big difference.

“Extra-virgin olive oil is generally the best choice for maximizing health benefits because it’s cold-pressed and unrefined, meaning it retains more of its natural antioxidants and polyphenols,” she explains. “This is the one you’re likely eating at a restaurant when they serve the bread.”

Regular virgin olive oil is also cold-pressed and offers similar benefits, but it has a slightly higher acidity level and milder flavor, while refined olive oil goes through additional processing that reduces even more of those benefits.

“I'd recommend reaching for extra-virgin olive oil whenever possible, especially formaking salad dressings, drizzling over vegetables, or adding to meals,” MacDonald adds. “It's an easy way to get the most nutritional value out of the olive oil you're already using.”

While, for the sake of the experiment, I drank it straight, MacDonald says that it’s actually not the most practical way to consume it.

“I'd recommend incorporating extra-virgin olive oil into your everyday meals instead,” she says. “Not only is it easier on digestion, but it can also help your body absorb fat-soluble vitamins from other foods.”

She doesn’t have to tell me twice. I’ll drizzle it on salads and roasted veggies, mix it with lemon (but for a dressing this time!), and add it to fish and whole grains.

“Using olive oil, a source of fat containing both mono- and polyunsaturated fats, in place of saturated fats like butter is another easy way to support heart health,” MacDonald continues. “It's really about making olive oil part of a balanced eating pattern, rather than treating it as something you need to incorporate each morning separately.”

While it’s not a downside per se, MacDonald points out that a typical serving of olive oil (a.k.a. one tablespoon) has about 120 calories and 14 grams of fat, so a two-tablespoon shot would total about 240 calories and 28 grams of fat. The TL;DR? While it’s a great source of heart-healthy monounsaturated fats and antioxidants, it’s also calorie-dense.

“Taking a shot every morning can add up quickly, especially if you're adding it on top of your usual diet—which may already include using olive oil or other fats in your meals and cooking processes,” she says. “Rather than thinking of olive oil as something extra you need to consume, I’d recommend using it in place of saturated fats, like butter or coconut oil, when applicable, as part of your everyday meals.”

I did notice changes in how I felt overall after taking olive oil shots every day, especially with my digestion. But given MacDonald’s advice that you can reap the same benefits from simply incorporating it into your everyday diet, I think I’ll retire the morning shots in favor of something I’ll actually enjoy—like O.O. on a salad or with a fresh piece of sourdough. I’ll get my daily dose the old-fashioned way, and reserve the shot glasses for my Friday nights.

PowerBank completes 3-MW New York community solar project

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Renewable energy developer PowerBank has completed the 3.26-MW Camillus community solar project in Camillus, New York, and has received permission to operate. Solar Advocate Development, LLC, the project’s owner and EPC, built the array on a capped landfill, which it acquired from PowerBank in spring 2025 in a $7.3 million transaction.

“A closed landfill is land that most people would write off,” said Andrew van Doorn, president and COO of PowerBank. “At Camillus, our engineers and construction partners turned it into a 3.26-MW source of clean power for local households. Reaching permission to operate on a site like this reflects decades of hands-on experience, and it’s exactly the kind of project where that experience matters most.”

The project will soon accept community solar subscriptions. Solar Simplified will manage customer acquisition and subscription services for this community solar project.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

Te Araroa: the boogeyman got me

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Every trail has its boogeymen and its icons. On Te Araroa you run into your first of each essentially at the same time. Kilometre 135.5 marks the base of the Raetea Forest. Notorious for its mud and for being one of the first places on the trail where people end up getting heli-lifted out. At the base of the hill sits the Krishna Sanctuary, where for a donation you can camp and get fed a massive curry meal by Abhay and Jaya, the owners of the sanctuary.

I hit this on day 5, after one and a half rest days and a resupply in which I bought way too much food and also deodorant since it seems like this trail actually has showers every couple of days.

On day 5 I woke up feeling like a nervous wreck and unsure what to do with my day. I knew rain was coming the next day and was worried about the already muddy forest. I decided to just go loosely with my original plan – to get to the Krishna Sanctuary and then make a call about whether I’d climb halfway up to the Raetea summit to camp with some of my friends. That led to my first time hitching, a mere two minutes into walking, a woman pulled up in her ute with her very cute dog and loaded us into the car and drove us all the way to the Krishna sanctuary. Meeting her and being at the sanctuary was like a full reset for my nervous system. I stayed the night there on the floor of a cabin, massaged my tight calves and nursed my remaining blisters from the beach.

By the time my alarm went off at 5:15am I was feeling good. A little nervous for this boogeyman of a hill but physically strong and ready for a big push. I was so excited to be off the beach.

The day was hard, but getting up the hill went quickly, I walked with two couples I had met the night before and they were fast up and down the hills. We laughed a lot as people slipped in mud and the boys sang and did gollum impressions. By the time we reached the summit I was feeling triumphant, this hill was not so bad!! But then the rain really set in and the descent began. Steep, deep mud for 8km. Eventually I lost the couples, preferring to not rush and navigate the mud alone. I got stuck up to my mid-shins and had to dig myself out with my poles. But overall I was feeling great, my usually-a-problem knee was holding up fantastically and I had only fallen once.

Then, about 5km from the end I slipped and my left leg folded under me and bashed into some sharp roots. It was one of those falls where your whole brain sets off an alarm and you have to count to ten before you can start assessing if you’re really hurt. The sharp pain from the sicks had me wondering if my sock had ripped (luckily no) and I stood and tested my ankle – it seemed ok so I kept walking. I didn’t have much choice.

By the time I made it out of the forest, the rain was getting lighter and I meandered through a misty field trying to find the trail markers.

I was so close to the gravel road to camp when I had my third fall of the day, walking down what I thought was regular dirt but was actually extremely slippery mud, toward a gate that would let me onto the road. I fell in slow motion, swearing at the universe. This fall didn’t hurt my body so much as my ego. I threw my pack over the gate and climbed over the stile before hobbling my muddy butt up the road to the campsite.

I was so incredibly happy to see my friends and the sun peaking through the clouds. After nine and a bit hours I had made it! There was a stream for washing and collecting water and I did both, using my muddy shorts to wash both themselves and my legs. I got my tent up and airing out, and someone gave me pre-boiled water for a hot chocolate. Everything was looking up!

But what goes up must come down I guess, and my left ankle started to swell. Turns out the fall and bashing it on those sticks did hurt. I massaged it and did my best to keep off it and by morning it felt walkable, so off we set.

This day was one of emotional ups and downs, it was supposed to be the chillest, easiest day of the section. Mostly gravel roads for 23km and a dairy (convenience store) 6km in. Unfortunately, my ankle was GRUMPY.

There was a mobile voting place at the dairy so I could vote right on trail and have an ice cream at the same time! The ladies at the voting place were so kind and I told them it was the first time I was able to vote in NZ so this was a big deal for me. I cast my vote then went to the dairy and bought myself an icecream and a canned iced coffee. Glorious.

The next 10km or so up a forestry road flew by. Then my ankle decided it was once again in a bad mood. I hobbled down the forestry road to reach a stream about 1.5km out from camp and stood in its very cold waters for ages to get the swelling down. It was gorgeous and a relief.

Camp this night was much more established, with a water tank, toilet and a shelter. I decided that if my ankle was still sore in the morning I’d stay, I had plenty of food, battery charge, and I knew more people would turn up the next day.

I felt a bit sad at this prospect but I knew I’d feel stupid if I pushed it and had to get off trail. The magic of thru hiking struck this night, and we had a lovely time chatting in the shelter and eating the whole block of Whittaker’s chocolate my friend brought. I got to see a ruru (a native NZ owl) for the first time ever. We all had a good laugh when someone’s tent got raided by a possum. It’s all ups and downs.

My ankle was, indeed, still sore this morning. One of my day-one friends decided to stay with me for the day as she also had tons of food and wanted to take some time to sort out her gear and the blisters on her toes. I managed to get some messages out to Fraser and the trail angel who was going to pick me up to let them know that I was okay but delayed.

We spent the morning slowly drinking our tea and coffee, and eating breakfast. I washed some of my clothes, read with my feet up in the air to help with swelling and munched on our over-abundance of snacks. To be honest, it was exactly what I needed.

Another group of hikers turned up in the afternoon and one was a physio (the trail provides!) she assessed my ankle and did a lymphatic drainage massage on it. She was a life saver. In the morning I felt way better but decided it wasn’t smart to continue on the rougher track ahead and backtracked to the road where I was picked up by a trail angel. This is NOT how I wanted this section to go, I really was looking forward to the river sections, but I needed to be smart and recognize that even with some improvement on my ankle, it was likely an overuse injury and needed real rest and to be checked by a physio in town.

To say I was disappointed is an understatement, I have never had an issue with my ankle like that so it really blindsided me. But taking a break is not quitting. And boy will it make for a good comeback story when I’m recovered and back on trail. For now, it’s several rest days and hoping I don’t get too stir crazy.

The whole album Grace by Jeff Buckley. I had never listened to it and Fraser told me it was essential listening. I listened to it while sitting in the sun in the Krishna Sanctuary garden, I loved it.

Honourable mention: that “just keep swimming” song Dory sings in finding nemo which got me through the last 3km of the Raetea forest.

Now I spend my time convincing my friends to do big walks with me and taking on overly-ambitious craft projects. This season I'm attempting a thru-hike of Te Araroa, starting October 2026.

Hyundai’s electric SUV finds some footing, with IONIQ 5 sales topping 33,000 in 2026

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The Hyundai IONIQ 5 remains one of America’s most popular EVs outside Tesla, with October sales up 21%.

While the Tesla Model Y and Model 3 still dominate the US electric vehicle market, accounting for about 50% of all sales through the third quarter, it was the Hyundai IONIQ 5 that ranked third.

The market has slowed since the $7,500 federal tax credit was removed last September, with several automakers reporting fewer EV sales than in 2025 when buyers rushed to claim the credit.

Hyundai responded by cutting prices on the 2026 IONIQ 5 by up to nearly $10,000 on some trims, with a new starting MSRP of around $35,000.

As the market shows signs of recovery, IONIQ 5 sales bounced back in October, rising 21% to 1,981 units.

Through the first 10 months of 2026, Hyundai sold 33,093 IONIQ 5s in the US, still down 23% from the more than 42,700 it sold during the same period last year, when credit was still available.

Hyundai said the IONIQ 5 helped drive growth as the company posted its best October in the US ever with 73,452 vehicles sold, a 5% increase YOY.

While Hyundai sold only 169 IONIQ 9s last month (-47%), sales are still up 49% through October, with nearly 6,700 units sold.

Hyundai’s hybrids set a new October sales record, rising 20% from last year and accounting for 29% of total sales in October. Through October, Hyundai’s total US sales are up 3% YOY at 770,916 units.

Since not all automakers report monthly sales figures, it’s hard to determine where the IONIQ 5 stands through October.

Earlier today, Hyundai’s sister brand Kia reported that sales of its three-row electric SUV, the EV9, were up 11% year over year in October. EV9 sales are now up 5% year-to-date, with 13,835 units sold through October.

The cheapest 2027 Hyundai IONIQ 5 SE Standard Range starts at $35,250 and offers up to 245 miles of range.

If you want more range, upgrading to the extended-range SE RWD model costs an extra $2,500, but you get up to 318 miles of range on a single charge.

What Are Local Movie Theaters Showing on Election Night?

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

What does an audience need from its movie theater on election night?

A provocation? A distraction? Something… else? Across our semi-united, Letterboxd-loving nation, new microcinemas are opening up, old movies are selling out, and local programmers are giving film fans tons of exciting reasons to hit the box office.

Also breaking, I guess: the U.S. midterms. (Go vote!)

This election cycle, the future of Hollywood remains an open question, as does the strength of our democracy. With control of Congress at stake, voters are debating what they want their country to become — and, in many cases, what they’re afraid it already is — to spectacular and exhausting effect. Related Stories ‘The Intertwining’ Review: Two Medical Students Uncover Buried Truths in Tense Psychological Drama Billy Evans Hosts AMA on (His Own) X Account: ‘You Can See Everything’ Subject on Not Being a Cocaine Fan, Going Full Frontal, and What His Cousin’s Thing Was

Boarding yourself up in a movie palace like one of Joe Dante’s gremlins seems sort of reasonable under the circumstances. Thick walls, emergency snacks, and no checking your phone makes buying a movie ticket on November 3 an intriguing proposition in the U.S.

IndieWire spoke with 14 theater operators, programmers, filmmakers, educators, and authors across 12 states, including California, Illinois, Massachusetts, Minnesota, Missouri, Nevada, New York, North Carolina, Pennsylvania, Tennessee, Texas, and Wisconsin. Their answers offer a weirdly hopeful view of communities looking for comfort, perspective, and somewhere to be when the results come in.

At Las Vegas’ Beverly Theater at least, the connection is intentional: Reese Witherspoon and Matthew Broderick’s “Election” shares the day’s lineup with “The Devils” and “The History of Concrete.”

“We program The Beverly as a reflection of what’s happening outside our doors,” founding creative director and CXO Kip Kelly wrote. “Politics has become such an absurd spectacle that a dark comedy about a high school student council election might actually be one of the more sophisticated conversations you’ll have that day.”

Meanwhile, at Nashville’s Belcourt, a popcorn bag oversees the transfer of programming power. The theater’s monthly Staff Picks series typically falls on the first Tuesday, with a name drawn from a bag determining the employee who chooses the film. This time, someone picked “Under the Silver Lake”: the L.A. conspiracy thriller that’s paranoid enough to make anyone start connecting the deep-state dots.

“It’s totally a coincidence,” programming director Toby Leonard told IndieWire, laughing on the phone. “We realized what we had done after the fact.”

AFS Cinema programmer Lars Nilsen was similarly blunt about his Austin theater’s approach.

“We just programmed it as a regular week.” Nilsen resisted recruiting “The Good, the Bad and the Ugly” into any kind of election commentary, instead describing its Italian vision of the Old West as “the kind of dream America that Italians thought they understood about America itself.”

That said, tonight’s gathering still matters to Nilsen — even when the movie AFS is showing has nothing to do with the election itself. He pointed to the Texas organization’s addition of a third screen as a vote of confidence in people continuing to show up for one another through repertory culture.

“I think very intricately and carefully programmed cinemas are one of the places where community is being reborn,” Nilsen said. “Thank goodness.”

For Harlem’s Maysles Documentary Center, building community and considering who gets to tell that community’s story is the program’s point. Executive director Kazembe Balagun deliberately selected “BLKNWS: Terms & Conditions” for an evening that’s ordinarily devoted to watching the news.

He said he wanted to ask: “How do we imagine ourselves, represent our communities and think about power beyond the ballot box?” Plus, “I just think it’s a dope film.”

The “BLKNWS” screening is followed by a neighborly gathering echoing the center’s other events, from World Cup sidewalk screenings to group viewing for the Knicks playoff games. “I think that it’s not just what we put on the screen, but also the rooms that we create,” Balagun said. “The party afterwards is intentional because right now we live in a time where our communities are so fragmented.”

At Pennsylvania’s Gap Theatre, Spectral Cinema programmers Adrianna Gober and Dan Santelli are confronting power as an ambitious intellectual team. Their 35mm selection, Bresson’s “The Devil, Probably,” follows a young man overwhelmed by disillusionment with the institutions around him.

“The film speaks so much to the feelings of alienation and powerlessness that a lot of people are grappling with right now,” Gober wrote. She described the struggle to find meaning and control as “a tug of war between hope and despair.”

Santelli sharply cautioned against allowing that despair to become another reason to give up. “In the current political climate, it’s all too easy to fall into a feedback loop of despair and resignation, whereby the recognition of one’s apparent powerlessness becomes a justification for further inaction,” he wrote, adding he’s “less interested in offering audiences an opportunity for catharsis than one for reflection.”

That distinction runs through other programmers’ choices, too. Just look at “The Devils.”

At Minneapolis’ Trylon, John Moret chose Ken Russell’s film to cap the witchy “Evil Incantations” series after its restoration was announced at this spring’s national Programmers’ Jam in L.A., which he co-organized. The Warner Bros. Clockwork news sent a room full of repertory devotees into a frenzy months before “The Devils” mania got properly started — and Moret saw the opportunity to send his audience at Trylon from the voting booth straight into Russell’s religious nightmare back then.

Every screening has sold out since, Moret said. And personally, he often spends election night at the movies: “I think that there’s going to be plenty of people who are looking for distraction.”

Art can offer distance while leaving a lot to recognize on a deeper level, but even outrageously visual, avoidance and arthouse rarely mix. Moret praised the magical appeal of Russell and “The Devils” specifically, and said great film “should reverberate truth in a way that connects with you, but also allows you to be at a different time and place and truly put it into perspective.”

At Brookline’s Coolidge Corner Theatre, the 1971 film is sticking around for the same reason: The worshippers just keep coming. Now in its third week, “The Devils” shares the November 3 calendar with “Voting Counts: Elections in Film,” an adult film class that proved popular enough to require a second section. The program was conceived for election season, but “Network” also got its turn on Election Eve.

“We want this to be everybody’s movie house. We want our audiences to feel at home here,” Coolidge Corner artistic director Mark Anastasio told IndieWire. “There has to be a variety of programming appealing to the diverse appetites of the Boston area.”

In Milwaukee, author Matthew J. Prigge is about to discover what his favorite movie feels like in a theater.

His book event at the Oriental Theatre includes a screening of “Dog Day Afternoon,” which Prigge spent years researching for his nonfiction “Dog’s Day.” But somehow has never actually seen on the big screen. The election timing was mostly coincidental, though Prigge recognizes the relevance of Sidney Lumet’s film — about a man robbing a bank to fund his lover’s gender-affirming surgery — in 2026.

The actual robbery, the writer cautioned, has a more complicated history than its Hollywood adaptation. Still, Prigge said, “I think that the movie works just because it’s such a human story and it gets to the actual people that are involved and it makes it relatable, it makes it very raw.”

He also noted that for one evening watching the 1975 title wouldn’t be work for him: “That’s a big deal for me to see it as a communal experience. It’s been a while since I’ve been able to just sit back and enjoy it.”

For Hyperreal Film Club’s Blake Williams in Texas, finding an audience to join helped make a new city feel like home — but things are “definitely tense” in Austin, he said. After moving south from Indiana, Williams discovered the organization through its eclectic programming and eventually joined their team.

Now, he says his closest LGBTQ friends in the local film scene are considering leaving the state because they feel unsafe. His election-night offering to a community he wants to keep together? “Hardball.” After a month of Halloween horror, Williams wanted the kind of crying that comes with a good ol’ sports movie, and Keanu Reeves coaching a group of kids offers a true tale of common ground through weepy comfort.

“You kind of want to see a change in these things happen and you hope that it’ll happen, but at least film can kind of make people forget about these things,” he said. “I think it’s cool that we’re able to offer our comforts to the community to put them at ease, even if it’s just for a few hours.”

Relief means different things to different moviegoers, and at North Kansas City’s Screenland Armour, co-owner Adam Roberts is offering the new “Wild at Heart” restoration, alongside animation studio Laika’s first-run “Wildwood.” The former’s booking followed the remaster’s pre-planned release schedule, but Roberts nevertheless sees David Lynch’s dark vision of American life as an prime excuse for escapism.

“We are highly engaged in our local community and routinely show political films,” he wrote to IndieWire. “I think for patrons looking for a distraction on Election Night, there is no better place than the cinema.”

Charlotte’s Independent Picture House discussed special election programming before deciding its existing lineup already had the assignment covered, too. Creative director Jay Morong, also a senior lecturer at UNC Charlotte, pointed to “Wild at Heart,” “The Devils,” and specifically “Digger.”

“These are all movies that are addressing very specific things that are in the consciousness of the voter at this moment in time,” he said. “Let’s just roll with what we got because this is what independent and art house theaters do.” IPH’s monthly filmmaker meetup also happened to fall on election night, giving North Carolina’s indie community somewhere to discuss results that could affect their livelihoods.

“We are constantly battling against the state government and the incentives and them cutting back on budgets here,” Morong said, lamenting a decline in area film and TV productions. “So the film community is very actively involved in who is winning these state and federal elections and how they’re [using their voice] or cheerleading for the film industry in North Carolina.”

In Los Angeles, moviegoers can leave the voting booth for something that looks suspiciously like… another one? At WHAMMY, Julie Orlick’s One Person Cinema fits a chair, a television, and a viewer wearing headphones inside a five-by-three-by-six-foot room. The resemblance wasn’t intentional, but when someone pointed it out, an election-night opening felt fitting.

“We all just thought it’d be a good fun thing to do after a serious day of activities,” she said.

The premiere offering features her erotic short “DÉSIR,” and unlike an actual voting booth, this one has a peephole through which someone outside can watch the person watching.

“The only people who get to experience it are the people that go inside the room,” Orlick said. “And I think that also brings a secrecy to the film.” The experimental artist said she built the installation as a final project at UCLA, conceiving a private encounter with intimate images she wanted to keep off the internet. With WHAMMY and TAPE, she developed a residency featuring other filmmakers’ work, too.

An accompanying screening of “Les fantômes du hard” in WHAMMY’s larger microcinema rounds out an evening with considerably more erotic material than the average polling place — and you can find Orlick’s One Person Cinema inside the Echo Park microcinema all month.

Alternatively, over in Illinois, audiences at Chicago’s Gene Siskel Film Center can travel the globe without leaving their seats. Jia Zhangke’s “The World” — set among workers at a Beijing theme park filled with miniature global landmarks — screens as part of “Contact Zones,” a public series and lecture class taught by School of the Art Institute of Chicago professor Daniel Quiles.

“We’re given a sort of official line that we’re a polarized country or that we have arch enemies around the world,” Quiles said. “How can art give us a different way of framing these things?”

The professor’s interest in globalization’s recent past grew from questions about what retreating from it might cost. The screening film’s he’s chosen bring students, museum curators, and other moviegoers together to consider their stakes seriously: “I think by watching a film like ‘The World, ‘we might see parallels to the underclass in our own country rather than seeing China as some big bad guy.”

Finally, among respondents to IndieWire’s latest Reddit inquiry, several welcomed a few hours away from the election. Being “expressly forbidden from looking at my phone” was a selling point for one; but another, invested in a tight race, couldn’t contemplate the disconnect.

Meanwhile, others had already discharged their civic duties to make room for a different national emergency: Godzilla. “I voted early specifically so I can see Godzilla Minus Zero on Godzilla Day without having to worry about making it back to vote on time,” one kaiju fan wrote.

In Washington, D.C., another moviegoer plans to see A24’s “The Drama” at Suns Cinema.

“I didn’t realize it was election night when I bought the tickets, but I love supporting Suns any day of the year,” they wrote in an email to IndieWire. With its would-be wedding mired in finger-pointing, the popular flick from earlier this year feels well-suited to the socially charged D.C. setting — even if this moviegoer’s turnout concerns are more personal than political.

“I will hopefully be going with my crush (fingers crossed) if they respond to my texts,” they said.

Binance breach puts custody at center of crypto infrastructure trade

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The $6.3 billion Binance hack could accelerate a shift in how investors value cryptocurrency companies, putting a greater premium on custody providers and the infrastructure needed to securely hold and protect digital assets.

Much of crypto's evolution this year has involved companies moving past their reliance on trading volumes and transaction fees, to focus instead on building the infrastructure connecting digital assets with traditional finance. Last Thursday's breach adds a new dimension, bringing cybersecurity, custody, compliance and balance-sheet resilience into sharper focus.

Importantly, the Binance breach was not an indictment of crypto itself, or a case of leadership-driven fraud, as with FTX in late 2022. Instead, Binance's leadership moved quickly to contain the fallout, protect customers and commit the exchange's own resources to cover losses.

On Tuesday, Binance CEO Richard Teng told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" that the company has replenished 98% of $6.34 billion in affected assets, and that it is still in the process of purchasing outstanding, more illiquid assets.

Management's response, alongside efforts elsewhere in the industry to boost confidence, helped prevent a security breach from escalating into a broader crisis of confidence. For example, Tether, Circle, Ripple and others moved quickly to freeze or restrict access to about $3 billion in stolen assets; Coinbase blocked transfers to compromised wallets and offered Binance direct assistance.

The hack could even help shape the next phase of the crypto infrastructure trade. The new winners may not just be the businesses with the most users, volume or assets under custody, but the ones that can also demonstrate they're able to protect those assets under extreme duress.

"Which company has the resources to invest in cybersecurity and protect customer funds? For a smaller company, it could be much harder because once these crypto companies become infrastructure plays, providing the well for tokenization … they need to have the compliance and control in place so that a similar hacking will not happen or can be minimized," Owen Lau, senior analyst at ClearStreet, told CNBC. "Larger companies have more resources that could give them some advantage."

While institutional investors have increasingly embraced digital assets, broader adoption depends on confidence that those assets can be securely held, accessed and transferred. The Binance breach highlights the importance of that infrastructure, and could increase demand for specialized custodians and platforms with established asset-protection capabilities.

Coinbase has operated a dedicated institutional custody business since 2018. Circle entered the custody business this year, after the U.S. Office of the Comptroller of the Currency granted it approval to operate as a trust bank. In the past few years, Ripple and Kraken have also launched custody businesses.

Still, smaller players compete in the space as well. Small cap crypto exchange stock Gemini Space Station has offered custody services since 2019. BitGo Holdings, founded in 2013, is one of the earliest players in institutional crypto custody and infrastructure, with a reputation for security and serving major institutions.

The shift could also drive consolidation as companies look to buy capabilities, deepen their infrastructure offerings and achieve the scale needed to compete, Lau said.

The more consequential structural shift may be the separation of custody from execution, according to Morgan Stanley.

"Crypto exchanges have historically combined trading, custody, and settlement within vertically integrated platforms, concentrating operational risk. Repeated security breaches could encourage investors to move assets toward independent custody while continuing to trade on CEXs," the firm's analyst Felix Stratmann said in a note Monday," referring to centralized cryptocurrency exchanges.

"This would preserve the role of centralized exchanges as liquidity and execution venues, while potentially benefiting regulated custodians, including banks, as institutional investors place greater emphasis on asset segregation, operational resilience, and investor protection," Stratmann said.

Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac rally as Ackman presses for conservatorship exit

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The midterm elections could be a turning point for the release of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac from government control, according to billionaire investor Bill Ackman.

Investors may agree with him, if the rally in both stocks is any sign.

The Federal National Mortgage Association, known as Fannie Mae, and Federal Home Loan Mortgage Corporation, or Freddie Mac, have each jumped more than 60% from Oct. 14 to Monday's close. The stocks were both up roughly 3% on Tuesday.

Ackman, the founder of Pershing Square Capital Management, has long pushed for the two entities to be released from federal conservatorship.

"The reason why it's not being touched right now is, you know, midterms," he said in an interview with CNBC's "Squawk Box."

Ackman, who owns both names through Pershing Square, believes Freddie and Fannie have the potential to increase "10-fold from current share prices," he said in his quarterly letter to shareholders, issued on Monday.

Fannie and Freddie buy mortgages from banks and other lenders, and then package them into mortgage-backed securities, known as MBS. Their operations are critical to the housing market, providing liquidity to banks and creating a secondary market for MBS.

The entities were placed into conservatorship in September 2008, during the financial crisis. The stocks were delisted from the New York Stock Exchange in 2010 and have traded over the counter since then.

Ackman wants the conservatorship to end, arguing the entities would be worth multiples of their current valuation if they were freed from government control.

President Donald Trump has supported ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie. But it remains politically and economically contentious.

The entities operate under strict capital threshold requirements and would need to be recapitalized, which could happen through issuing common equity. However, the Treasury Department would need to figure out an exit plan for its roughly $388 billion ownership stake in the entities through preferred securities and warrants.

Congress may also need to play a role, passing legislation to restructure the government's role in mortgage financing and home-loan guarantees.

Nonetheless, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent recently said it could happen before Trump's term ends, telling 2Way's "The Morning Meeting" podcast that the administration will tackle the ownership structure "next year, the year after." He said affordability will be guiding the decision.

One point of controversy is whether ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie would increase the spreads on MBS over Treasuries, effectively making mortgages costlier. Bessent has sought to reassure the market that wouldn't happen.

"Whatever we do is going to be guided by making sure that the spread on [agency] mortgages … over the Treasury does not increase," Bessent said.

Ackman argues that privatizing the GSEs would help insulate them from political meddling. "Fannie and Freddie are these massive, enormously profitable institutions where whichever political party controls them…if they're still in conservatorship, can influence the outcome of elections," he told CNBC.

In one scenario, he explained, Democrats win the White House, conservatorship is still in place, and Democrats push Fannie and Freddie to lower credit standards in order to expand home ownership and affordability.

"If they're going to move more aggressively into borrowers with lower credit quality, for example, to achieve a political objective, that's going to have an impact on trillions of dollars outstanding mortgage securities," Ackman said. "It's going to have an impact on overall credit spreads."

MBS investors want certainty about how the entities will be run, he noted.

"That only gets resolved in a world in which they exit conservatorship, they become publicly traded companies that are owned by institutional and retail investors," Ackman said. "We set the rules. In conservatorship, the president can do anything."

The Citadel Project is a Thief-inspired dark fantasy, survival-horror lite stealth-em-up with some lovely shadows set in a medieval megastructure

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

You know, given the whole stealthy element of Thief-like immersive sims, I'm surprised there haven't been more of them with horror elements. There's Gloomwood, sure, but it's not as common as I'd like. That in turn then makes me curious about The Citadel Project, an immersive sim with some survival-horror elements set in a nasty, parasite-infected dark fantasy world, all apparently made by just one person.

In The Citadel Project, "the last line of defense built by the Ancient Kings in an attempt to preserve what remains of humanity in the face of the Great Storm." Some kind of evil force is ruining it from the inside, and you have to make your way through a medieval megastructure to figure out what happened. You aren't anyone special, "just a simple prisoner who manages to escape from a cell deep within the Citadel." Truthfully, the story sounds fine enough, I'm more smitten with the vibes put on display.

The shadows are what sells me, for the most part. They're dark and moody, looking plenty ripe for hiding in. And look, if you hand me a megastructure to explore, I'm going to do so. I'm also a fan of your main weapon, a crossbow. Not enough games with crossbows if you ask me! There's all sorts of bolts you can use with it, like a fire bolt that, obviously, sets things or enemies alight, a rope bolt to help you quickly climb or swing out of danger, a decoy bolt that makes a lot of sound to distract with, or a moss bolt; I'm not sure what that one does, my guess would be to dampen your footsteps and make it easier to sneak up on unsuspecting baddies.

Said baddies also appear to be infected with some kind of parasite, the severing of which appears to come in handy when trying to take them down. Overall, The Citadel Project likes a neat little im sim with just enough going on to make me curious to explore further. Which I might do, when I get the chance, as there is a demo available on Steam. Alternatively, I could just wait a week, as the game is out in early access November 11th, just over a week away. You can wishlist it on Steam right here.

JetBlue raises bag fees for third time in a year as fuel costs bite

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

JetBlue Airways has raised the price of checking a bag for many customers for the third time in less than a year as high fuel costs continue to eat into the industry's profits.

Checking a first bag for travelers booked in economy will now be $50, up from $45, if purchased more than 24 hours before departure during a nonpeak travel period. If it's paid within 24 hours of departure, the fee will be $60. During a peak period, such as during the holidays or the summer, it will be $55 if purchased more than 24 hours before departure, up from $49, and $70 for within 24 hours of departure.

There are exceptions to the checked bag fees, such as for customers who hold a co-branded JetBlue credit card or who hold elite frequent flyer status.

"We're adjusting checked bag fees to help offset rising fuel and operating costs," JetBlue said in a statement. "This helps us keep our base fares competitive while giving customers the choice to pay for a checked bag only if they need one."

The price of jet fuel, airlines' biggest cost after labor, has roughly doubled this year since the start of the Iran war in February, costing airlines billions and driving up the price of tickets. Jet fuel was going for $4.44 a gallon on Tuesday for the U.S. Gulf of Mexico, according to the U.S. Energy Information Administration.

Other airlines often follow when one carrier raises fees. Major U.S. airlines did so earlier this year after JetBlue raised its bag fees.

The changes were reported earlier by the Thrifty Traveler blog.

Some might argue that the person who already has everything is the hardest to shop for. But we disagree. It’s an opportunity to throw practicality out the window and find something so offbeat, clever, or downright whimsical that they’d never think to buy it themselves. That’s what makes shopping for them so fun. Consider it your chance to give something unexpected, such as the 12 gifts below.

They might have all the gear, but have they ever made their own gear? When the weather is crappy and the daylight wanes, this DIY zipper pouch is just the thing to keep them busy. Ripstop by the Roll provides the Dyneema fabric, YKK coil zipper and slider, and drawcord (thread not included), and it’s up to them to put everything together. Choose from three sizes.

It’s wide enough to wrap multiple times around the neck, or once around the shoulders or head. It’s back! After a six-year hiatus, your climbing-obsessed loved one will be delighted to know this beloved magnetic locking carabiner is in production again. Unlike locking carabiners that require users to twist a screw tight, the Magnetron locks automatically when the gate is closed. It’s compact and lightweight enough to roll up and carry in a daypack or toss in the car for a tailgate.

We guarantee the recipient already owns a camp mug—or two or three. They probably wouldn’t spend $110 on another one themselves. But that’s exactly why you should. Everyone has a camera in their pocket, but how often do those videos make it out of the camera roll? Without a playback screen, the person behind the lens has to trust their instincts, just like back in the day. But a USB-C connection makes transferring footage easy. Surely your loved one already has a pair of tried-and-true trail shoes. But do they have these trail shoes? The collaboration between Québec footwear brand Norda and climbing gear maker Black Diamond was one of the year’s most exciting partnerships, and this limited-edition shoe is its crown jewel. Here’s your chance to give someone something they might have admired from afar but wouldn’t necessarily splurge on themselves.

All their gear has to go somewhere, and hopefully in an organized manner. The RUX Waterproof Duffel Box keeps everything contained in one durable, grab-and-go bag for impromptu trips to wherever. Its zipperless roll-top design expands or compresses to accommodate whatever they’re packing, while interior soft organizers keep smaller essentials from getting lost in the shuffle. Collaborations make especially good gifts, particularly when they’re limited to just 500 pieces. For the person who loves a good vintage find, this is about as good as it gets.
